A. Conan Doyle, whose father was an artist, was born in Edinburgh in 1859. He began to write at the early age of seventeen, while studying medi- cine. He wrote about sixty short stories in the ten years before be became known through his widely- lead "Sherlock Holmes" 'tales, and he has since given to the reading world such sustained efforts es" The White Company " " Micah Clarke," "The ifiefugees," and "The Great Shadow." Conan Doyle is a close student of old romances, a great admirer of Scott and Fennimore Cooper, and as lecturer on George Meredith. whom he places at the head of contemporary novel writers.

There is a story going the rounds just now(a New- I castle contemporary says) which is causing much amusement in certain colliery districts. The late manager of the Newbottle Co-operative Stores, who has absconded with nearly ten thousand pounds of , the society's money, took no little interest in re- ligious matters, and was a local preacher. Preach- ing on the last occasion but two it is said he appro- priately chose as the subject of his discourse the following words :—" A little while and ye shall not see Me." On the nest occasion he based his re- marks on the text, "Ye shall seek Me and shall not I find Me." His concluding discourse was founded on the passage', "The time of my departure is at hand.' Since then many of tits hearers have found out the literal truthfulness of the words selected.

There is now no question that if the House of I Lords take the extreme view of the contracting- out question they will be warmly backed up by Mr. Chamberlain and his immediate friends. I under- stand that Mr. Chamberlain has personally ex- pressed the opinion that Mr. M'Laren's amendment did not go lar enough and as there is no doubt that Lord Salisbury is acting in this matter in consulta- tion with the Unionist leaders in the House of Commons, we may look for an attitude of no corn- promise. There is a rumour, however, which I give tor what it is worth, that the Duke of Devonshire is personally by no means disposed to an extreme view, although be may allow himself to be swayed in this matter, as in so many others, by the feelings of the other political leaders with whom he is act- ing ht concert.

A movement is afoot to stop the interference with the old woods and fragments of old woods of the New Forest, and with what are known as the King William 111. Woods. The old woods are only 4.600 Jinxes out of a total acreage of more than 60,000. The modern plantations are about 17,000 acres. In the older woods more than 2,000 trees have been cut during seven years under the excuse of fuel rights. There has also been interference with the young growth, and in many instances new and fanciful varieties which are not indigenous to the forest have been introduced. These particular woods, it must be remembered, are almost the only if not the only examples in this country of old woodland that has grown naturally and has remained in its natural condition. They are full of historic memories, and they are full of beauty. It is hoped that ucli arrangements will be made as regards fuel rights that there will be no excuse for cutting a single tree in the old woods again.

There is much dissatisfaction among the Labour members of the Labour Commission at the way in which the report is being compiled and submitted to them for approval. No regular discussion has yet been possible on the summary of the evidence prepared for the acceptance of the Commission, ' owing to the piecemeal manner in which it has been placed betnre them. One section after another ' has been placed before meetings of the Cimmission with only very brief notice, and has been declared to be passed although no opportunity of suitable discussion has been afforded. The fifth section of the summary of the evidence is to be discussed at the next meeting of the Commission, on Tuesday. The only notice the members have had of the terms ot this section was sent out on Saturday last, with an intimation that notice of any amendments to it were to he lodged before the following Tuesday. That is to say, the Secretary only allowed members two days in which to prepare amendments which he required a week to digest before placing them betore the Commission.

This procedure is strongly resented by the Lab- our members and their supporters, and it is their intention to raise a protest next Tuesday against the attempt to " rush " the report through. The view taken of the evidence in the summary must necessarily be a forecast of the recommendations and form a basis for them. It is therefore of the utmost importance, if the recommendations are to faithfully represent the views of the Commission as a whole, that the fullest opportunity should be given for their discussion.

Seldom—perhaps never—has the United Kingdom Temperance Convention had so brilliant and suc- cessful a meeting as that which took place on Wed- nesday night in the Convent-garden Theatre. It may be, writes a London Correspondent, that the surroundings had something to do with the result. The interior of the famed Royal Opera House is in itself, when brilliantly illuminated by the electric light, as it was on Wednesday evening, an attrac- tive sight; but when floor, balconies, and galleries are filled with an interested audience, all the re- quirements necessary to make a success are pro- vided. Sir W. Lawson, who presided.is a recognised humorist, and he did not lower his reputation in that respect. For example, he made a casual refer- ence to Lord Randolph Churchill. The audience; laughed. "Why do you laugh?" asked Sir Wilfrid "I regard him as one of the wisest men of the day. I always call him Daniel. Don't you know that he speaks as one who has come from the lion's den?" —an allusion of a double meaning to Lord It. Churchill's presence with the Brewers' Association and to his experiences in Mashonaland, which was readily appreciated. and fittingly rewarded by a roar of laughter. But, apart from the jokes, Sir Wilfrid Lawson's speech was a very powerful argu- ment tor giving the control of the liquor traffic into the hae.ds of the people in the different localities, and in support of Sir W. Harcourt's " Liquor Traffic (Local Control) Bill." In conclusion, he said that the Government should deal with the matter before another twelve months were over, dnd so clear the way for a better, brighter, and happier future for our nation.

Lady Henry Somerset was manifestly a "persona grata" with the audience, and her rising was the signal for a protracted and hearty outburst of cheer- ing. High principle and lofty sentiment were the characteristics of her speech, but its eloquence also fairly charmed the meeting. One burst of eloquence electrified the audience. Lady Henry Somerset had been pointing out what a great interest women had in this matter. Suddenly she exclaimed, "God save the people! And how does he save them? He saves them by placing in their hands the machinery of law, by which they can saye themselves." So impassioned was the delivery of this passage that every lung seemed to Jain in the great cheer that arose when it was finished. Mr. A. Birrell, M.P., the clever author of " °biter Dicta," seconded the resolution which her Ladyship moved, which re- joiced at the recognition by the Government of the magnitude of the evils arising from the traffic in intoxicants, and the importance of giving to the in- habitants of localities power by direct popular vote to prevent the continuance of the traffic in their midst against their will. Another resolution ex- pressed gratification at the introduction by the Chancellor of the Exchequer of the Liquer Traffic Local Control Bill, and urged the Government to place it in the forefront of their programme for next session. It is scarcely necessary to add that both resolutions were carried with enthusiasm.

THE EXAMINER

LADIES' LETTER

MT DEAREST NELLIE,

Frocks for little people continue to be very quaint and pretty, while at the same time there is a good deal more variety in the style of make than has been the case for some time past. Very small children are, of course, still often seen in the long- popular amook dress, but many of the newest frocks have fu/1 bodices to which the skirt is sewn, or plain shortwaisted ones after the Dutch mode, while the older girls' dresses are often of the Prin. oease type, or are made with long full basques, although belted bodices are still vice fashionable. A useful costume for a girl of 11 or 12 is made of electric-blue serge, made a la Princesse, and having each seam piped with dark brown velvet, the skirt is edged with a narrow frill of velvet, and from the collar-band falls a pleated shoulder. cape of serge bound with velvet. A wide sash of shot brown and blue eurah encircles the waist, and is knotted at the left side with long ends, and the sleevcs have a large puff of serge at the top, the rest being of velvet.

A still more serviceable and equally stylish costume is of brown face-cloth; the skirt is trimmed with four double rows of narrow black braid pub on in waves, and the round-waisted bodice has full fronts, the fullness being arranged In small box-pleats, held down by three double rows of the waved braid, arranged at intervals between neck and waist. Titie belt and collar are of black velvet, and the sleeves of serge with braided cuffs. Another dress has a full bodice with a band round the waist, and it has a yoke and collar or corduroy velvet, and is trimmed with stripes of velvet. The shot moires of the present season need no word of commendation. These ex9nisite fabrics are manufactured in many and various combine. Lions, the most beautiful being, I consider, those of pearl and salmon and green and mauve. Other moires are shown, having their shiny surface be- decked with tiny satin spots. I say tiny, for these spots are not larger than a pin's head. Indeed, all the new patterns are small. The enormous designs on brocades, and, indeed, on all materials, have gone out, and are not likely to appear amongst us for several months to come. Basques are shown on many of the new bodices, but the shapes differ greatly, and it %ILI take some time for the fashion to settle down and for any particular style to become fashionable. One type of bazque is put on separately, and is generally of "the round type, cut to fit over the hips, and set in full pleats at the back. A very pleasing example of this fashion can be added to a full or a tight bodice, as it looks equally well with or without a belt. If the material is sufficiently wide, each half is cut entire, and the front portion is hollowed out in cape style, which brings the part over the hips on the bias, and ensures a close fit at the waist, while the edge sets out well without hang- ing in folds.

Stylish seems to be the most appropriate word by which to describe the new millinery. True, many of the new hat shapes are fiat and uninterest- ing looking, and when untrimmed have anything but a stylish appearanci, but the milliner's fingers quickly effect a compiSte transformation. The trimmings are arranged to stand high, and are fixed in front. A very stylish confection for a young lady is a large hat in the newest shades of felt, trimmed with ribbon and velvet mixed. It has also a bow and shades quills in front. As the season advances, the pelisse becomes every day more to be respected. A pelisse it should be, with either a fur cape or vest, a full skirt and a bait round the waist. With one of these and a short sealskin with a very full basque, you may consider your coat wardrobe complete for quite two months. I have met some delightful pelisses with vests and sleeves made entirely of fur, cloth capes outlining the vests and falling full to the waist at the back.

Every second person you meet wears a sable or its equivalent roend her neck, those of this year being disingashed from those of last year by their "headlessness," although you do here and there come across women who have failed to grasp the fact that it is not pretty to hang a complete corpse round their necks. A new short sable boas is corn- poed of two whole skim.. Fur is as fashionable a trimming for children's frocks and coats as for those of their elders, and very charming for girls over 10 are Ruesian blouse costumes of green or °ion ‘in on brown f ace•cloth, trimmed round skirt and deep full I aeque with brown fur, the wide belt being of veket, as are in many cases the sleeves. For indoor wear—indeed such a frock as this is soiliotty smart to wear at a quiet party—apretty drees has an accordion-kilted skirt of dark green vicuna, the full bodice is of shaded green vicuna, with green velvet belt and collar, and the upper part of the sleeves is com- posed of four frills, the three upper ones being of shaded vicuna, and the lowest of the plain kind, kilted to match the skirt. Yours lovingly, Jusras.

Mr. E. F. Benson, author of the now farnousnovel,l " Dodo," says that he prefers women to men as subjects for character drawing,and the reason he gives is because women are mpre complex and inconsistent than men. The minister of the parish of St. Cyru.,Vicotland, has the distribution of what is known as the " marriage money," being the interest of a sum left by a Mr. Orr, of Bridgeton, to the oldest, the youngest, the tallest,and the shortest woman, belonging to the parish, who may happen to get married in the parish church during the year.

SATE COAY, DECEMBER 9, 1893.

At a reception given last week by a well-known Broad Church clergyman (§p a London correspondent says), a lady of his family, attired in the stereotyped accordian pleated Lett". Lind skirts, performed " serpentine dances " and jigs for the entertainment of the guests. A famous novelist has been plunged into grief by the Ardlament tragedy. Ellen /slathers, the author of " Cherry Ripe," is a sister of Mrs. Hambrough, mother of the murdered man, to whom she was much attached. Miss Mather alters very little though she has grown rather deaf. She retains her fresh colouring and beau- tiful auburn hair. Sister Woodhull, says the New York " Recorder," is at her logic again. What an interesting family she is part and parcel of ! Her father called his daughters after the States Virginia and Tennessee, reserving Victoria for across the water visitations. It Mrs. Martin would give a biography, social, pelitical, financial, and personal, she could furnish a book which no student of human nature could afford to be without. Miss Annie Corless, who has just carried off the third prize at the Milan Conservatoire, is the first Irishwoman to achieve this distinction. Her success is the more creditable to the young contralto because the number of competitors was unusually large, and because for many years past the exhibition has been won by Italian stu- dents. Miss Corless, whose voice is of unusual sweet- ness, compass, and volume, is the daughter of Mr.Thos. Corless, of the Burlington Hotel, Dublin.

A Nice Dish for Supper. Boil a large teacupful of rice in a good quantity ft f boiling water, for just a quarter of an hour, strain it,and wash it well in cold water, strain it quite free fio,n the water, and leave to drain while you melt a large piece of dripping in a frying-pan. Fry a teaspoonful of parsley and one union chopped floe; if you have any pieces of meat or fish, put them into the frying pan to warm. Then slice in two tomatoes. When all these are boiling mix in the rice, stir all together until hot, and serve at once.

Pancakes Without Milk,

Put a breakfas-tc"u—pin-l—of flour into a basin, break two whole eggs into the flour, stirring the eggs and flour wed together. When the eggs and flour are well mixed moisten the mixture with a teacupful of cold water,beat this up well with a wooden spoon, put a piece of dripp- ing in the frying-pan. When it frizzles put in three table- spoonfuls of the batter, see that it runs all over the pan; when set, turn it over with a knife ; when brown roll it up, and keep hot while you fry the remainder of the batter, Serve with treacle or sugar and lemon.

Pith Pie.

Buy a large haddock Or-Pial-ce-,w- Ipe it, and cut in four pieces, peel four large potataes cut them in thick slices, and lay them at the bottom of a pie-dish, sprinkle them with pepper, salt, a little chopped parsley and onion, las the pieces of fish on the potatoes, then another layer of potatoes. Make a paste with two ounces of dripping and half a pound of flour ; half-fill the dish with hot water, cover at once with the paste, bake in a moderate oven half-an-hour.

WIDNES WOMENS:LIBERAL ASSOCIATION. , • ner';rgitl-r;i'W Rfql.l7ol Under the auspices of this association, a public meeting was held in St. Paul's Parchial Rooms, on Monday evening. Mrs. Stewart Brown presided and there were present on the platform :—Mrs. O'Reeffe, Miss Deacon and Miss Hilda Deacon. The room was well filled. . .

Mrs. Brown, whe had an enthusiastic reception, in the course of an introductory address, said she was sure all present heartily sympathised with the sufferers of the coal strike, especially with the women and children' They, as Liberals, owed a debt of very deep gratitude to the Liberal Government, and especially to Mr. Glad- stone, for having stepped in at the right moment and arranged a conference, which was held under the presi- dency of an impartial chairman, Lord Rosebery. (Applause.) The conference had been successful for the present; at any rate, in reconciling the miners and mas- ters. They all rejoiced that it was a victory for the men who were fighting for a " living wage." (Applause.) Mrs. Brown then called upon Miss E. Robinson, of Southport, who delivered a lecture on the subject of " African Wars." She said we had no sooner left off stealing the Africans from Africa than we commenced to steal Africa from the Africans. We too often forgot that these people were human beings. They might be savages, but they had bodies to feed and hearts to love. War was a barbarous and sav- age custom, not an honour but a disgrace to the land. Dealing with the Zulu war, which was commenced in 1879, when a Conservative Government was in power, and although it was hoped when the Liberals came into power that the war would have ended, it was said there were certain considerations of policy which could not he overlooked and the quarrel could not be settl:d. Miss Robinson thought no policy ought to interfere with matters which were just and right. In an eloquen, manner the lecturer described the proe,ress of the war and showed how Cetewayo wished not to go on with the war but the English Government would not listen to the proposal and went on until village atter village was des. troyed and thousands of men, women and children slain. Eventually King Cetewayo was captund and brought to England, after which it was decided to give him a por- tion of his land back, but as his people's dwellings were destroyed and thousands of natives killed, he died of a broken heart. Miss Robinson appealed to the women present to do their utmost t-> abolish warfare. Ow reason why she was in favour of women's suffrage, was because women would have an influence in the cause of temperance, peace and righteousness.—On the motion of Mrs. O'Keeffe seconded by Mr. FL Wade Deacon, a vote of thanks was accorded to Was Robinson and a similiar compliment Was paid to the chairwoman.—The lecture was illustrated by lime light views.

Uldene gazed at the picture breathlessly, she Gould nob tell why. Suddenly Neddy gave her a nudge. " Dene,' she whispered, shrilly, under her breath, "who is that gentleman leaning against the marble pillar to the right of us? For the lasb five minutes he haa not taken his eyes off your face." Uldene turned her dark eyed from the picture she was contemplating to the person indicated, and met the fixed gaze of the stranger bent upon her. What was there about this person, wh.2m she had never met before, that fascinated her, made the blood creep through her heart with a deadly sensation, like that which fills the heart of it fluttering dove that falls under the steady gaze er a serpent. The floor seemed to rock beneath her feet, the grand pictures in their gilded frames to whirl around her, and the Air to grow dense and stifle her. "I don't know who he is, Neddy," she said, faintly. "I do not like being stared at so rudely. Let us go—let us leave the place." They moved with the dense crowd; Nodal could not resist the impulse that once possessed _Lot's wife—to look back, and see what had become of the dark-browed stranger. "Oh, Uldene I" she cried, "this is grovving romantic; he must have fallen in love with you -- never dreaming a young girl like you could be married. He's actually following us." "Oh, Neddy, I am horribly afraid of that man,' f gasped Uldene. "I —I don't know why, but 1 am. A premonition of coming evil seems creeping coldly over my heart. There was anything but love in the gaze that met mine; I read in the fiery eyes that looked into mine deadly hatred, such as one reads in the eyes of a relentless bitter foe. Come, let us hurry away, and elude him in the throng." Neddy agreed, and they were soon on the pave- ment. The tall, dark-bearded stranger reached the door just in time to see the two slender figures enter their coach and drive away. "Can you tell me who that beautiful young lady is—the dark one, I mean, who just entered that coupe ?" he asked of one of the attendants 01 the academy. "I have heard the name, but I cannot call it to mind just now, sir," answered the man. comes here to the weekly Thursday receptions. can tell you this much, though—young as she isv she's married." "Can you find out her name and address for me?" he asked, slipping some money into the man's hand. "Yes, sir," said the attendant, touching his OF respectfully to the liberal stranger. "Call all time after next Thursday, and I will have it for you." The attendant smiled as he looked after the tall, aristocratic figure until the stranger vanished froo sight. 'lt didn't seem to make any difference to him."' my hint that the beauty is married, and is not jot him," he muttered.

ANCIENT POTTERI.—We les re, on the authority of Vitruvius, who wrote In the Augustan age, th". the Romans then made their water-pipes of potter's' clay. The people, who introduced a knowledge of the usbful arts practised by themselves wherever their conquests were extended, established potteries 3n Englard, where among other artiotes, similar .: water-pipes were made. Some of these about a ~ century ago, were dug up in Hyde Park. They were fcund to he two inches in thickness, and were firmly' ~ jointed together with common mortar mixed with oil. THE BEST XMAS PRESENT.

Christmas bells will ring in family reunion. Young and old look forward with great joy and it is the keen- est endeavour ol every °se to pass this fête in the family circle. When gathered around the Xmas tree any absent member will be painfully missed, and a sigh given for those who are away. Nothing, however, interferes with the joy of Xmas more than if one of the family is stricken down with disease, and recovery to health for Such member, would no doubt be most appreciated of all 'Cams presents for the whole family. This valuable present will be found in the timely em- PloYment of Warner's Safe Cure, whereby sorrow would be changed into joy. Nearly all diseases of the human 1.3.3.43' depend upon disorders of the kidneys liver and 'llgestive organs, being the laboratory for the whole or- ganism. Warner's Safe Cure is a sure and reliable remedy for the cure of these disorders, and ensures good health. Follow the example of Mr. Edmund Parker, 2, Wil- son's Place, St. Paul's, Bristol, who solemnly asserts that Warner's Safe Cure is a most valuable gift. His own Word.: "I had been a great sufferer, in fact confined to 1113' bed for eleven weeks: during my illness I had been attended by my club doctor, then by two local doct•lrs, and afterwards at the hospital. They all told me my case hopeless unless I went under an operation, to which would not consent. I was troubled with bloating after 179eals ; furred tongue; bad taste in the mouth; throb- Ping pain in the temples; restless nights; shooting pains !a the right side of back, loins, legs and feet; undue thirst, and palpitation of the heart. I then procured a bottle of Warner's Safe Cure and commenced taking it, arid, to my gratification and astonishment, the symp- _otos and the pains began to lessen. The hemorrhage iron which I suffered disappeared after three days' use of the Safe Cure, and this was followed by excretions of !and and gravel, which continued until the kidneys were ea and health completely restored. lam now able to resume work—a thing I never expected to do again."

IHE FA MINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 9, 1893.,

time would soon come when they would have to make extensions. The minutes were confirmed. CEMETERY COMMITTEE. The minutes were ot a formal ctuu acter, and were confirmed, en the motion of Alderman HaanisoN, seconded by elder-flan ASHTON. REMOVAL OF THE TOWN HALL WALL. " DIRTY LOW POLITICAL FEELING." Ala meeting of the Town Hill, Park and Baths Com- mittee, held on the 23rd November, it was resolved "That the wall in frent of the 'Town Hall and the Re- creation Ground be taken down and set back to make Sankey-street 60 feet in width." Councillor BOLTON, in moving the confirmation of the minutes of the committee, said the members of the Council would notice the resolution with regard to the baths. It was the intention of the committee to make expenses connected with the baths during the winter smaller than they were summer. At the same time, the efficiency of bioe baths would be kept up. Then there was the resolution with regard to the removal ef the Town Hall wall. This matter had been before the Council on previous occasions, and had been passed since or twice. Several MEMBERS.— No, no. Councillor BOLTON.—It has been passed. Alderman SurroN.—B% the committee. Councillor BOLTON.—But it was passed by the Council (" No, no.") It was passed by the casting vote of the Mayor. (" No, no.") I say yes. Ald,rman Surrox.--Well, it does not matter. Councillor BOLTON, sontinuing, said he thought the time had now arrived for the wall to come down. (Hear, hear.) One portion of it was in a very bad condition; the stones were soft and crumbling, and it would take a good sum to repair the wall. The question was whether it would be better to take down the wall or repair it. He would not like to have the wall in his backyard. (Laugh- ter.) It was not a credit to the Town Hall, and it was too high to enable them to see into the Park. The com- mittee required a lot of stone for a fence for the baths in Legh-street to the Drill Hall, and the Town Hall wall would do to use for the purpose. (Hear, hear.) There was another matter to which he wished to refer, and it was this—that the time had come when they might widen Sankey-street to 60ft., and it could be done with- out much cost to the ratepayers. The entrance to the goods yard at Bank Quay was a very bad one; so bad indeed that at times it was dangerous for horses to make use of it when dark, but by widening the street they could improve the whole district. The committee did not propose to touch any of the entrances at present ; or put a gateway in the centre ; the time for that arrange- ment and for the spending of the money would be taken into consideration later on. He did not think that any practical economist need fear the cost, as the committee would be able to carry out the improvement at a cheap rate. This was wintfr, and they wished to have the per- mission of the Council to take down the wall, and then, when they had unemployed in their midst, the work would be carried out. (Hear, hear.) Councillor Dr. CANNELL seconded the passing of the minutes. Councillor R. T. FAIRCLOUGH proposed as an amend- ment that the minute relating to the removal of the wall be not carried out. The committee wished to pull down the wall, but they did not say what they were going to put up. (Hear, hear.) If money was to be spent, he should like to know in what manner. He thought that after the plans for the enlargement of the Town Hall had been subreitted,the Council %you'd be in a better position to know what the character of the entrances should be. (Hear, hear.) Councillor FLOOD seconded the amendment. The question of the removal of the wall had been discussed from time to time, but he had never yet seen any neces- sity for its removal except in the case of an important and comprehensive scheme. He objected to this con- stant tinkering. The Council had not been told what the probable cost would be. No one was more favourable to a public improvement than he was, but if they wished to improve Sankey-st., let them commence at the narrow- est end, and where the traffic was most congested. There was another matter which should be considered. There were more obstacles that day to the proposal than ever. They were now suffering from a heavy rate. He was told that the cost of taking down the wall would be from £7OO to £BOO, and he objected to the carrying out of the work to satisfy a fad. They had been told that the Town Hall had to be improved. Why, therefore, should there be patchwork ? Let them improve the Town Fall first. Councillor Mum said the committee had well digested the question for some time, and all ratepayers who had been consulted were delighted that the wall was about to be taken down. Councillor Flood was entirely wrong with regard to the cost. Mr. Longdin had assured them that the cost would be £250. Councillor GREENING was in favour of the removal of the wall, and he was pleased to see that the committee had had the courage to put the minute on the agenda paper. If the cost was only about £5OO nine-tenths of the people of Warrington would vote for the removal. (Hear, hear.) Councillor FIEATON said the committee had carefully considered the question, but they wished to have some instruction from the Town Council with a view to pre- paring the necessary plans, and to put the cost in the estimates for the ensuing year. It would be a wise action on the part of the Council to sanction the removal of the wall, as the widest entrance to the Park at the present moment was only 4ft.—(" Oh, oh ")—and at the corner of Crosfield-st. a proper and appropriate entrance could be made to admit the passage of a horse and carriage. Councillor HILL supported the passing of the minutes. SOllle of their narrow-minded friends thought the expense would be great, but no improvement had ever been carried out without expense. Councillor LONGTON supported the amendment. It did not seem that the members of the committee were agreed as to the cost of taking down the wall. How would the wall be removed, and what would be the ex- pense ? The Borough Surveyor had said that the cost would be £250. Alderman PLATT.—The Borough Surveyor shakes his head. He 'won't have that. Councillor LONGTON, continuing, said he should have thought that Councillor Bolton would have stated the cost of the work. It seemed that there was a diversity of opinion about it. (" No, no," from several members, and Councillor Roberts, " Quite right.") Reference had been made to the stone being soft and crumbling, and vet Councillor Bolton had said that it was wanted to form a new wall in Bath-street. (Laughter.) He hoped that the new walls required by the Corporation were not going to be made out of soft and crumbling stones. (Hear, hear.) One statement which had been made was inconsistent with the other statement. (Hear, hear.) Alluding to the recent municipal contest, Councillor Longton said that during the compaign he did not meet one amongst his constituents who objected to the course he proposed to take in opposing the re-, moval of the wall. Alderman SUTTON said he should support the action of the committee, as he firmly believed it was the wish of the public that the wall should be removed. One day he (the speaker) met a stranger who was en- quiring his way to the Town Hall. He volunteered to show him the way, and he was surprised when he was told that the Town Hall was concealed by the wall in question. He said he should have thought the building was either a prison or a lunatic asylum. (Loud laugh- ter.) That was the view strangers took of the Town Hall. One of the foremost properties of the town was ' confined within a prison wall. Councillor BENNETT was of opinion that the gentleman to whom Alderman Sutton had alluded WEIS perfectly right. (Laughter.) He could not conceive of any councillor of sane mind objecting to a ref 'irm so absolu- tely necessary, and which the whole town, in spite of what Councillor Longton had said, had wanted for many years, namely, the removal of the wall. (Hear, hear.) The question had been discussed year in year out, ever since he could remember, and no valid objec- tion to the pulling down of the wall had been raised,ex- cent that of expense. He was not blind to the fact that the rates were tco high, but sometimes it was possible to be penny WiSP and pound foolish, and if there was any object for which it wasjustifiable to spend the rates the removal of the wall was one of them. (Hear, hear.) Ile should vote for the resolution because he approved of the principle. What was to be put up in place of the wall was a secondary consideration ; personally he thought it would be better to have a field open to the street, although there wore several alternatives which could no doubt be discussed afterwards, if the resolu- tion was carried. There was a good deal to be said for the palisade scheme, of which the plan was before them that nighealthoueh he should prefer to see the Southport plan adopted, the ground laid open,and the place mado beautiful and artistic. But these were all matters of de- tail, and the great thing that night was to carry the re- solution. In conclusion, he contended that they must make streets for the great town that was coming when the Ship Canal was opened, when trade had developed, and when Warrington had multiplied three or fourfold. In]ether towns, the Town Hall was a building which long ago would have been thrown open to public gaze. kHear, hear.) Councillor SHAW GREEN : Are we in a position to set a portion of the recreation ground to the front of the street, or to be added to the street, without a special application to the Local Government Board The MAYOR: It will be necessary to apply for sanc- tion. Councillor SHAW GREEN said the Chairman of the committee had left the Council very much in the dark in I connection with this matter. A-great deal of his (Conn. Green's) opposition had been removed by conversation with members of the Council, especially with one of the Aldermen, but he did feel that the present was the most inopportune time possible to bring the matter forward. Councillor HuTottesoN : It always has been. Councillor SHAW GREEN said that personally he should like to see a bit of green sward, and that kind of sight was growinz less year by year in Warrington, owing to the density of the atmosphere, but he should be very sorry to see the little bit in the main thoroughfare re- moved. The rates were just sow Bs. 4d. in the Z. They had postponed the payment of a sum equal to another Pl. Next year they would have to provide for that 4d. Time KtVOR You mean on account of the small- pox?

Councillor SHAW GREEN Yes. If you propo.‘e to spend £430 in addition to the 4d. we shall have te find next year, and when you hear it hinted by A Id. len in Platt that one department has spent £350 more Lb in its estimate,with three months to runet is time that econo- my was exercised. (Hear. hear.) Alderman Stereos; : That has not been proved. The MAYOR : Order, order. Councillor HEATON : It is usual for that committee to exceed its estimates. (Laughter.) Councillor Sliaw GREEN : Then how is it possible to make our financial arrangements if money is to be spent in excess of estimates? We shall never be out of debt. We have serious obligations before us, and it is the duty of every committee to make smaller estimates than they have been accustomed to make for the coming year. Councillor ROBERTS supported the amendment, and said the description which had been given of the Town Hall being very like a prison of a lunatic asylum was not far from the mark. He would certainly like to see the Town Hall improved off the face of the earth, and something more modern provided in its place. If the people of Warrington were so anxious to have the wall removed how was it that not a single member of the Council who had men elected since the year 1890 had ventured to refer to the subject in his address to the electors, with the exception of Councillor Bennett? It was perfect rot to say that the people of Warrington wanted the wall taking down. (Cries of " Perfect rot" and "No, no.") Perfectly parliamentary. (Laughter.) Aide' man Sutton had teld them, and he ought to know —(laughter)--that it was not proposed to carry out the work at once, but the resolution stated distinctly that it was proposed to take down the wall the whole length of the front of the Town Hall and the Recreation ground, and to widen the street to 80 feet. It was more a ques- tion of cost than anything else. To take down the present wall, to set it back, to widen the street and make it a good one—and it would have to be made a good one—would mean an expenditure of £2.000. (Laughter.) That would not be much in excess of what the actual expenditure would be. (Hear, hear.) He objected to the work being carried out in shreds and patches. If the committee came before them with a complete scheme, and with the expense carefully pre- pared, the Council could consider the whole matter on its merits, and not be deluded into accepting the princi- ple. Talk about buying a pig in a poke! There never was a more flagrant attempt to hoodwink a Council into a delusive position than the present attempt. Talk about costing £250 or £400! Everyone knew that both those sums put together would not cover a third of the total cost. (Hear, hear.) They had that day in the Town Hall far more officials than was fair to them. (Cries of " Hear, hear," and " Nonsense.") He (Coun- cillor Roberts) was not a doctor, and would be very sorry to express his opinion in contradiction to the very valu- able opinion of Dr. Cannell, but he should not like to be one of those officiels who was compelled to work at the Town Hall under existing circumstances. Councillor Dr. CANNELL : There are rooms unused in this building to-day. Councillor ROBERTS : There are cellars. Councillor Dr. CARRELL : There are rooms upstairs. Councillor ROBERTS. continuing, said the officials ought to be accommodated properly, and the time would come very soon when they would have to provide ad- ditional accommodation for the servants of the Corpora- tion, including those who had to be removed from the Water Works office, and who were temporarily lodged in these premises. They wanted a new Bridewell. Com- plaints had been made about it from time to time by the Government Inspector, and it was possible they might lose the grant if they did not make an alteration in this respect. They wanted a new Police Court, and the hos- pital accommodation was utterly insufficient for the re- quirements of the town. The present hospital was built at a time when the population was certainly 20,000 less than it was now, and no extensions of any moment had been made, while cases of enteric fever were being treated at home owing to want of accommodation. Their present indebtedness was £568,656. It was quite true that a large sum was represented by remunerative under- -1 takings, such as the gas and water works, but they had I an unremunerative debt of £108,450 and they ought, therefore, to be careful before they added a single half- penny to the rates that could, in any sense, be put off. Councillor Devies had not intended to make his debut on a question of that kind, but alter what had been said he did not consider he should be doing his duty if he gave a silent vote. As Councillor Roberts had said, they ought to put off minor questions like the present. lie contended that this part of Sankey-et. was the best street in the town. (Hear, hear.) Councillor Bennett had said he could not conceive of any sane man object- ing to its removal, while he (Councillor Davies) thought that those who voted for it would be more fitted for a lunatic asylum. I shall speak fearlessly—as fearlessly as Council- , ler Roberts doeS. I don't care a fig what anyone thinks. If I think I am doing right I shall do it, and I think this is the wrong time to remove the wall. (Hear, hear.) Alderman PLATT Faid the question had been pretty well thrashed out; and after the remarks and facts which had been put before the Council by Councillor Roberts he did not think anyone coted have much difficulty in making up his mind what to do. There was a very special reason why they should be careful not to incur expense. No one present could say the wall could be removed without expense. They were now as a borough at a very critical period in their his- tory. Next year the Ship Canal would be opened, and manufecturers of various kinds who wished to establish works near the canal would in the course of the next 12 months, be looking out for the best spots. , Warrington had many advantages with regard to rail and water ways, and one of the first questions thet would he asked would be "What is the amount of the rates ?" The answer was" 6s. 4.d. in the £, and a pro- bability of an increase." Was not that a very serious matter for any manufacturer to face. A PLYMOUTH MIRACLE.

A LIFE SAVED IN THE £0 F.

INTERVIEW WITH THE DISTRICT SECRETARY. (From the " Weekly Mercury," Plymouth.)

In the centre of the busiest thoroughfare in Plymouth —The Union-street that joins the Three Towns of Ply- mouth, Davenport, and Stonehouse—is a fin e open space, surrounded by business premises, call( d the Octagon. And incontestably the handsomest building in the place is the Foresters' Hall, a comparatively new erection, where the local business of that vast provident society is transacted. Upon the ground floor, to the left of the entrance, is the secretary's office, and it was here that a representative of the " Weekly Mercury" found the abject of his special visit to the establish- ment.

Mr. J. W. Northey is the General Secretary of the Devon andCoriawall District of the Ancient Order of Forreaters. As soon as the reporter presented his card and Mr. Northey, the secretary, under stood the nature of the business, he left everything else to conduct the inquirer to his private apartments at the top of the building. Mr. Northey was in high spirits,, and any father I would be whose daughter had just* recovered, as if by miraculous means, from an illness which seemed likely to be fatal. The facts of the case may be;briefly stated I thus :

Mies Northey, for a considerable ,time had been very ill. She suffered from weakness, less of flesh and gen- eral debility, and, to use her own words, often felt so miserable that she would like to die. This state of things lasted for months. Several doctors treated her, but they seemed to do her no good. She gave them up. Just then her father who was in a terrible condition of despairing grief, happened to see a notice relating to a wonderful remedy, which purported to have saved people from the death that seemed likely to overtake his daughter. Be read the account of what Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People had done, was convinced of the uselessness of going any further with the prescrip- tions that the doctors had given her, and resolved to make a trial of the Pills. Miss Northey had been obliged to give up work of every description some time before, had no appetite, and was gradually wasting away. Anything was better than a continuance of that, and Mr. Northey at once set off for some of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, which the girl commenced to take. She had not finished one box before she was conscious of great improvement. She took seven boxes in all, and by then had recovered her former appearance of health, her strength had returned, the feeling of enervation was gone, and she could eat her food as heartily as before she was taken ill.

Mr. Northey made the reporter the possessor of this information before the latter proceeded to interview the victim herself.

As they ascended the handsome staircase, Mr.Northey asked,—

" Rave you ever suffered from influenza, I mean the real thing, --Ia grippe ?" Then you know how horribly weak an attack of that sort loaves a man ! It is a bit awkward for a busy secretary to be unable to walk across the room. Now, I had a touch of influenza. and, do what I would, I could not get rid of the effects of it. But when I saw what good Dr. Williams' Pink Pills had done my daughter,l thought they might do me good too. I took the contents of one box, and the result was marvellous, lam in better health now than I have been for two years before."

"Well, you are looking fairly robust," was the res- ponse. Mr. Northev led the way into a neatly furnished sitt- ing room, with a number of engravings upon the walls. It was nut long before Miss Northey was ushered into the room. When one has been informed that a girl has been suffering four months from an insidious illness which has reduced her to a skeleton and made her wish to die, he does not expect to see a young lady with a charming colour and a sprightly manner enter the room. So that the reporter was constrained to ask, " Is this the right daughter, Mr. Northey ?"

" Oh, yes, this is the one," said that gentleman, smil- ing. "Does not look much like a skeleton now,does she? "My dear, this gentleman has come to ask you about the benefit that Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have afforded you.,,

"Oh !" replied Miss Northey, " if that is the case, all I can do is to praise them beyond any in edicine I ever heard of. It it had not been for them I hardly know where I should have been now. Certainly I should not have been in fit condition to receive visitGrs. 'She does not look like a dying girl now, does she?" asked Mr. Northey again. He seemed so delighted with the cure that had been effected that he was full of the comparison between his daughter's condition then and a few weeks pieviously. "You should have seen her a month cr five weeks ago," said ho; "you would not have known her for the same person."

" What was your complaint, Miss Northey " inquired the reporter. "Well, I may say that I was suffering from several complaints, and was generally much debilitated. I had indigestion, and I was so weak that I could hardly move. I was tired of everything and everybody ; I felt every morning as if I had no desire to live through the day." "Then your father saw Dr. Williams' Pink Pills ad- vertised ?"

"Yes, and he ordered some of them. That was a good day's work for me, indeed." "Did you feel any improvement rapidly?" "Yea. It was marvellous; long before one box had been exhausted I was much better. Four days after I commenced to take them I said they had done me more good than all the medicine I had taken previously." . . . .

" And you had taken a good deal ?" "Yes. Several doctors had me in hand from time to time since we came to Plymouth, but in my opinion neither of them understood the cause or the nature of my illness. All they could say was that I was suffering from general weakness, and that they could not curo me. They said they might be able to prop me up for a time, but that was all." "And that was not what you wanted ? "

"No, I wanted to be cured. I wanted to recover the old sense of freedom and strength,—the old colour and the old appetite. It was not till I took Dr. Williams' Pink Pills that I lost the sense of danger. Then, as I told you just now, I had not been taking them a week before hope returned. I continued to use them, and that is why I can talk to you like this now. I can recommend Dr. Williams' Pink Pills to anyone who suffers as you have suffered P'

"Yea. I shall rcommend them to everybody I know. They are simply wonderful." Then the reporter took his leave, and the last words he heard from Mr. Northey as he went into the street , were : " They are a wonderful medicine." Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are recognised as one of the greatest modern medicines, a perfect blood builder and nerve restorer, curing such diseases as Rheumatism, Neuralgia, Partial Paralysis, Locomotor Ataxy, St. Vitus' Dance, Nervous Headache, Nervous Prostration, and the tired feeling resulting therefrom, diseases depending upon Humours in the Blood, such as Scrofula, Chronic Erysipelas, etc. Dr. tVilliams' Pink Pills re3tore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health, and are a specific for all the troubles peculiar to the female sex, while in the case of men they effect a radical cure in all cases arising from mental worry, over work, or excesses of whatever nature.

The pills are manufactured by Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, of 46, 'Holborn Viaduct, London, E.C. (and of Brockville, Canada), and are sold only in boxes bear- ing the firm's tradpmark and wrapper at 2s. 91. a box, or six boxes for 13s. 91. A descriptive pamphlet :sen t free by post on application. Bear in mind that Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are never sold in bulk, or by the dozen or hundred, arid any dealer who offers substitutes in this form is trying to defraud you, and should be avoided. Ask your dealer for Dr.Willlarns' Pink Pills for Pale People, and refuse all imitations and substitutes. . .

Dr. Williams' Pink Pills may ba had of all chemists, or direct by post from Dr. Williams' Medicine Company from the above address. The price at which these pills are sold make a course of treatment cornparstively in- expensive as compared with other remedies or medical treatment.

SOCIAL, POLITICAL AND LITERARY

NOTES.

There is unmistakeable evidence of a strong re- action in the Puritan life of our coentry, in favour of the theatre. The disregard and objection to stage plays hitherto is now being superseded by the wiser policy of exerting an influence for their re- form, and it cannot be doubted that such a course will have a good effect on the managers and direc- tors who are responsible for the stage productions of to-day. It would be idle to deny that there has been no cause for complaint in the past. The asso- ciations and surroundings of the theatre have not been of a character which co-opel the support of many good-minded people, but the change ot the palst few years shows a vast improvement in this resperct, and also what can be done, if a sound and healthy opinion is only cultivated.

It is also a pleasure and satisfaction to record an extended interest in the best works of the writers of English literature. This will greatly assist and I develop an appreciation of dramatic lepresentations of a higher class, which are now, we are glad to say, becoming frequent. The debasing influence of Italian comedy, which began with Machiavelli and Ariosto prior to the Elizabethan period, on English theatrical life, is now almost imperceptible, and ' there is reasonable ground for assuming that in the near future, the stage will be raised to such a stan- dard as will merit support from the most Puritan- nica . l amongst us. It questionable whether a sufficiently wide distinction is drawn between theatres and the more common music halls. The prevailing idea has been to consider them under one group, and consequently, the fierce denuncia- tions of ungenerous critics have been flung broad- cast at theatres and music halls alike, without even acknowledging a dividing line. The names are enough to indicate the type of amusement offered, and this we ought to recognize.

There has just been issued from the press a neat volume of sermons and addresses by the Rev. C. F'. Aked, of Liverpool, under the title of "Changing Creeds and Social Struggles." The book is very welcome breathing as it has a treshness and inspira- tion of which we are much in need. The vigorous tone of the writer and the fearless attitude he adopts in discussing such questions as" The Gospel for the day," and particularly "The 8.b1e," in addi- tion to the stirring addresses on social and labour questions under the heading of " A Crime against the Nation," and " Garth the son of Beowulph," , form a distinct contrast to the highly flavoured platitudes of many pulpit orators. To recommend all who are interested in social work to purchase the book is small praise; to read its pages and study its arguments will repay any person who is desirous of acquiring a knowledge of important topics, written in a manner that. will merit the approval of broadminded men every where.

Mr. Frederic Harrison, the Positivist leader, in lecturing in London on Sunday last, on " Industry as it should be," made out a very good case indeed in support of his particular gospel, from his own stand-point. He contended that Positivism was the only Utopia which wtnt to the root of our defective social system. Ile complained bitterly that selfish- ness in human nature was responsible for the chaos and y,overty in the world; and charges rich and poor alike, Collectivists and Socialists, employers and employes, with cultivating selfish desires, thereby frustrating any attempt to combat the present evils of our industrial system. But when Mr. Harrison reaches this point, lie becomes less certain In his dogmatising, and with vague sugges- tions as to the invaluableness of a religion which he terms "the religion of humanity," he leaves us to meditate on the possibilities of its successful application.

If Mr. Harrison had laid out his proposition for the regeneration of humanity with anything like mathematical exactness, it might have been of ser- vice to many poor afflicted souls. Instead, he de- nounces men of every class and creed, except his own, but ungenerously abstains from showing them how this spirit of selfishness may be eradicated. It may be that the Positivist philosophy explains how this can be done, but strugglers on in the sea ot life will derive cold comfort from being told that the cause of their own misery is within them,with- out an accompanying instruction as to the method of extermination to be adopted. To mildly suggest to drowning men the getting into a boat to save themselves, when they have neither the necessary power or assistance, is equivalent to Mr Harrison's denunciation of selfishness in human nature, with- out appending even the shadow of a remedy for its removal.

The Parish Councils Bill is making steady pro- gress in committee and under the excellent direc- torship of Mr. Fowler, it may be expected to justify our utmost expectations. It would appear, however, that many opinions are held as to what powers should be granted to such Councils; and doubts are already being expressed as to the genuineness of the approving words of Tory legis• lators. To possess Local Government with powers such as the Opposition would bestow, would not justify the time that's being spent on the measure, and the Government may be congratulated on their ' persisting in retaining the clauses which will make the Councils powerful and influential bodies. The question of public charities is not the least of many important matters that come under the Bill, and the compromise which has been arrived at, will en- able a majority of parish trustees to carry out the wishes of their local constituents.

THE F.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE CHUM

A largely attended P.S.A. m-..ting was held in Wycliffe Church on Sunday afternoon. The chair was occupied by Mr. J. R. Voting who dslivere i a brief address on Thrift," a subject which he considered was not too well understood. It was one of the lessons to be learnt from the recent distress. He had come in contact with people who out of a small weekly wage, had saved suffi- ciently to keep them from asking relief during the three months they had been out of work, whilst utkers who had earned considerably more money were asking relief at the end of the first week. LABOR TROUBLES.

BY MR. H. WHITHAII.

I see from my ?apes this day that peace once more reigns amongst the colliers. It has been a long struggle, and many a home must have become a thing a the past. I have watched it pretty closely as it has made its weary way through the weeks and months, and have often wondered what would be the outcome. One thing was clear, the land- owner, the colliery owner, the coal distributor, and the independent gentlemen of the Mr. John Peers pattern, would never go one meal short, or lose one hour of their summer's stroll over the mountains. But what of the manufacturers and the workmen? what of the colliers? One needs tu keep a pretty tight grip on the pen when writing of this matter, especially when one thinks of the remarks touch- ing the conduct of the colliers, and the advice ten- dered to the destitute by those whose bread and butter was assured. I know it is no use making these poor pitiful re- marks. No good will come of them. And yet, is there no lessen to be learned ? Shall we not gather something from the past? Does experience count for nothing? I think it is allowed all round that this,the great- est labor trouble the world has ever seen,has been, in its essence, a lock out, not a strike. Let us make a comparison here. Since last June there has existed here in the States the most serious commercial panic, America has ever seen. Thousands of work- men have been thrown idle. Hundreds of steam engines have been in the hands of mere caretakers. It like the one at which lam engaged, usually employing over 3,000 people, now employ lees than 1,000. What has been the cause? No one seems to know, or worse, everyone seems to know, and yet all differ! It is enough to say that I have never seen a solitary instance where the workman was blamed,unless it may be in the sense that at the last Presidential election he voted for the Democrats and a sort of qualified free trade,instead of voting for the Republicans and out and out protection of the McKinley type. Thus it is clear that the two most serious labour troubles of the 19th century have been brought about by the men of "light and leading," and not by the ignorant masses. Our trouble is on yet, and likely to be for some time; yours is over, I hope, and may be discussed. The reason why the reduc- tion in wages was insisted on appears to be that the demand for coal, such as is used, exceeds the supply which is furnished by an increased number of persons who are engaged in mining it. In five years there has been an increase of 129,335 persons in the collieries. It is reported that there has been an increase of 24 per cent, in the workers while the out-put has increased only 8 per cent. The price of coal has increased to such an extent that the general industries have in many cases found it impossible to carry on production successfully. If the present wages are paid colliers, trouble must follow in the industrial circles. Three features are here apparent, either reduce colliers' wages, or, reduce factory, and mill hands wages, or, declare the business men bankrupt at once. Poor Old England, what a fix she is in ! Let us pile on the agony yet a little more. Coals must come down in price because England does a large export business. How can the Indian market be kept? Native coal, though inferior,will force it out if present prices are maintained. Then as to China, with more coal than the whole of Europe, and cost- ing at present little more than se. a ton, and millions of people who would be glad to get six- pence a day of twelve or thirteen hours. Verily here are formidable competitors. What you have nearer home, in Germany, and Belgium is well- known and there are forces at work which if not watched, will make the wage question in which, a formidable one to grapple with. No single trade can hope to grapple with the wage question. Like the bundle of sticks we all rise or fall together, we are only strong in our unity, and right doing. I have before me a long statistical report showing that wages in Germany arein most districts steadily decreasing. ,The best class of artizans can barely earn .C 1 a week. In quite a number of the trades where the workmen have families to maintain. there is a large deficiency which has to be made up by the wife and children. To make the position more complicated, I should point out here, and it should be borne in mind, that the German railway system, and the methods of moving minerals, and paying royalties are all arranged with a view to help commerce, and not to injure it as in England. In Belgium the workmen are very sad Anar- chists. They are now contending that they have a right to vote the same as their employers. They are also presumptuous enough to ask for half a day's holiday on Sunday afternoon, very wrong, this. This is a little discursive, but I thought it might throw a little light on the subject. Now let us get back to the colliers. They re- fused arbitratien. They also refused to allow any one else to work in their places. Why should arbi- tration be accepted when their wages were already at the lowest subsistence point. It was not a Board of Arbitrators that was wanted. Poesibly a board of conciliation might have done good. These men have struggled hard for years to rise in the social scale. If anyone sneers and alludes to pianos and dog fights, the collier may point to the Ches- hire squire's cock fights and to the ten thousand and one wasteful and senseless pleasures of the rich. Don't *suppose for a moment that the Almighty looks only amongst the colliers for his angels. The collier and all of us workmen think with Mazzini who laid " untiring emphasis on the necessity of a standard of comfort which should set men free to think of higher things than how test to wage war against poverty and want." Yea we are getting ' tired, fearfully tired of this everlasting grind, and for these reasons the " scab " when becomes along must be helped to keep the pace rip until he gets out. of sight. The British worker has every thing against him, and he will have to go under unless.—well, unless he does something heroic. Germany's exports into British markets are increasing enormously ; British exports to markets hitherto held by the British merchant are steadily, year by year, decreasing. German workmen's wages are near fifty per cent lower than English workmen's wages. It is all there in a nut shell. Now what is to he done ? For years a few of us have kept pegging away at the royalty question, .hoping thereby to create a healthy and active opinion thereon. The coal traders of the kingdom have complained that high royalty rents and a high range of railway rates and charges prevent them from entering into competition with other countries to the extent that they would otherwise do. It was stated, and, I think by a gentleman connected with Pearson and Knowles Co., that individual firms dare not take this question up as they would most certainly be prejudiced when mak- ing bargains with coal owners, and railway com- panies. I urge that this question should cease right here being merely academic; it must become practi- cal. It will never become practical until you change the color of your members' politics, and show more common sense in the selection of your local law makers. I suppose some one will say I have no right to interfere. It is no use saying this, as I said a few weeks ago, this globe of ours is shrinking wonder- fully. A few weeks ago coal was sent from America to England. True, it was the wrong sort, but we can send the right sort. It is cheaper to take goods lacross the ocean than it is to cart them through the streets only a few yards, We are sending ship loads of hay, because your farms are of no use. The question is worth a little of the working-man's thought. It is worth while fighting this long and bitter fights about a United Kingdom that cannot suppert its population. It is time trades unions took a more active part in educating their mem- bers on all matters. Wages are affected more by indirect than by direct causes. Causes remote and unseen produce sudden results. Fifty years an influences were at work in Ireland that are to-day producing the direct results. I have no hesitation at all in saying that quite a number of the opponents to the Ilome Rule Bill welcome it as a something over which they can fight, and thus keep the people's attention away from matters of vastly more importance to working men. More than this I believe that had Gladstone carried his miiasure, these people would have Bought out a pretext for war. This will certainly follow the passing of the measure unless the people get more united, and then the sorry spectacle will be seen of men shedding each other's blood to preserve what don't belong to them—the land. What is before England is apparent to all thinking men, and I cermet eon- elude with stronger words than the following of Mr. Leonard Courtney, M.P., "It is conceivable. that in the course of commercial rivalry between this and other nations, our supremacy in the pro- duction of coal may have to pass away from Eng- land, just as supremacy in the production of tin has passed away from Cornwall. If this is to be the ) case—and the production of coal is now less pro- gressive end inclining towards the stationary state

THAT old bone of contention The Park Wall. "the Town Hall wall "was again thrown down in the -Warrington Council Chamber on Tuesday evening. The Town Hall, Park and Baths Committee proposed " that the wall in front of the Town Hull and the Recrea- tion Ground be taken down and set back to make Sankey-street sixty feet in width." Similar pro- Posals have been before the Council on former occasions and have been rejected; on Tuesday night the Conimittee fared better, for their sug- gestion was adopted by eighteen votes to eight. It will be seen from our report that the debate on the subject was of a lively character. In the Council, as in the town, the division of opinion as to the advisability of taking down the wall is very 'narked. That division is not upon political lines, though an ill-advised attempt to make it appear ao was made by Alderman HARRISON. As a mat- ter of fact, although the majority consisted of Conservatives, the amendment for the rejection of the proposal was moved by a Conservative, Gum- 'oilier R. T. FAIRCLOUGH, and was supported by two Other Conservatives,Councillors DAVIES and LONG- TON. There is, in fact, no need to impute political motives to those who are opposed to this fresh expenditure of public money. There are several other reasons to account for their action. In the first place, many people are of opinion that the Pulling down of the wall would not be an im- provement but the reverse. To people holding that opinion, the spending of several hundreds of Pounds upon the scheme, with the rates already l_tt a high fig Ore, must seem a flagrant waste of ratepayers' money. And, surely, they are entitled to take that view without being accused of" dirty 10ar, political feeling." Then, again, there are nianY ethers who, while disposed to concede that the wall might be removed with advantage are strongly convin cod that there is other public Work which demands precedence of this, and that until the borough finances have been placed ,upon a better footing no work should be under- taken that is not absolutely necessary. As Coun- Oilier SRAW-GREEN very appositely pointed out, although the rates are six shillings and four- Pence in the pound—high e,ough in all conscience the Payment of a sum equal to another four- Pence in the pound will have to be provided for ' nezt Year. The replacing of the present Park Wall, if it is to be carried out next year, will mean the adding of another penny or twopence to the feurpence mentioned by Councillor SHAW-GREEN. This is a prospect which, we believe, will not be relished by the ratepayers of the town. The ques- tion will have to again come before the Council, when the Committee proposes a definite scheme, and it is not at all unlikely that, by that time, there will be such a revulsion of fooling that the majority of the Council will be on the other side.

ILLEGAL REMOVALL OF FURNITURE AT RUNCORN. At Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday, William Helsby was summoned by Thomas Moore, of Regent-street, for illegally removing goods. The defendant rented a house ,at 3s. per week from the complainant. He was 153. in arrears and during the night of the 29th November he removed goods worth £3. Helsby knew that Moore intended to distrain. Defendant said he had been out of work 13 weeks, and he intended to pay the rent when he got employment. The articles were not paid for and I Ihe was afraid of them being taken away. A fine of £1 and costs 9s. 6d. was imposed. Numerous wreaths were sent as a mark of respect and esteem. you want your WATCH HIPAIRED by skilled workmen take it to PRITCITARD'S. - Adtt. RUNCORN SONS OF TEMPERANCE. At the usual meeting of the above-named Society, on Saturday evening, under the presidency of Mr. Thomas J. Holt, a paper was read by Mr. Daniel Bisbrown on "Moral Courage." The writer said he was not going to speak of the courage displayed on the battlefield, but that which was shown in the conflicts and temptation of everyday life. He held that this was a higher type of heroism, and it was to be daily seen in the humbler walks of life. They were not to conform to custom, when conscience said that those customs were wrong. It was very easy to float with the stream, but effort was needed to be loyal to conviction. Men with a principle must dare to stand alone. The path of goodness was not t made easy. They had need to offer the schoolboy's prayer scratched on the desk "0 God help me to fear none but Thee." If they studied the history of every martyr and reformer in all ages they found that the spirit that lived in them made them strong and true. Not a day passed, which did not give them a chance to display the heroic spirit of these men. There was a dis- cussion in which the following members took part: Messrs. George S. Sutton, W. R. Ltttler, and Thomas Cooke. A vote of thanks was passed to Mr. Bisbrown for hie paper.

TEMPERANCE MEETING. The monthly meeting of the local branch of the Church of England Temperance Society was held in the National Schools, Church-street, on Monday evening. There was a small attendance. The Rector, who occupie4l the chair, said there were large attendances at the previous series of meetings. Then there were attractions, and he thought at the time.that more came for pleasure than for good. The Rector gave a reading fi om a book by Sir Robert Ball, after which Miss McCarthy sang "Molly Bawn," and Miss Ridgway gave a selection on the piano. Mr. E. J. Parr, Liverpool, was then introduced, and delivered an able lecture, lie spoke strongly of the evils of intemperance, and said that the only way one could be quite safe was to "leave strung drink alone." Mr. Parr interspersed his address with several short and well-told stories.

SOAKING RAIN PROOF OVERCOATS, yet porous. 555., C. JACKSON HOLMES, Oeneral Outfitter. Market Gate, Warring- t. ORFORD HARRIERS. On Saturday the Orford harriers visited Earles- town to keep their engagement with the Merles- town harriers. The latter club have been very successful this season, having defeated Herwich, Leigh,llighfield, and Bolton Haugh clubs in succes- sion. This engagement on Saturday was the first one the Orford men have taken part in,and it was, therefore, no mean task they tackled, as with a weakened team (through influenza) and the fact of Souch, of Salford Harriers, the northern cross country champion, being found in the ranks of the Earlestown runners, the chance of victory seemed almost hopeless. Notwithstanding these drawbacks, however, the Warrington men ran splendidly, and managed to obtain a victory by three points; a very small margin indeed, but very satisfactory taking everything into consideration. Souch, of course, was an easy first : ORFORD. --9 Total 3 Tot II 42 AVERY'S ScaLta.—Agent GARLAND. Bridge-street, War- rington. SERIOUS FIRE AT CROFT. Shortly before midnight on Monday, what re- sulted in a destructive lire broke out at the farm buildings ef Mr. Thomas Sanderson, Smith Brow, Croft. About ten o'clock an inspection of the farm buildings and outhothes was made as usual and everything appeared safe. The fire was detected at about 11-45, however,Police Constable Callaghan, who is stationed at Crott, ran to the farmhouse of Mr. E. W. Sankey, who, through the National Tele- phone Company, is in communication with the Leigh police Station. The L'igh fire Brigade was telephoned for, and the men promptly turned out and proceeded to the fire with the engine and appli- ances. The fire spread rapidly before the arrival of the brigade, and the glare attracted a large crowd of people. The brigade made every effort to stem the progress of the fire, and in a hour or so had accomplished this, but not before a great damage had been done. Fortunately the cattle and horses were rescued, and the brigade was instrumental in saving the farmhouse from the flames, which by their efforts, assisted by the police and many willing helpers, were confined to the barns and other out- buildings. The fire was not completely got under until about seven o'clock on Tuesday morning. The damage is roughly estimated at .£l,OOO which is covered by insurance.

The case at the Warrington County Court, in which Mr. Wood of Asliton-in-Makerfield sues Mr. Caldwell of Warrington' for alleged false imprison- ment on the occasion of an action in the Warring- ton Borough Court over a case of horse dealing, should have been celled for its adjourned hearing on Thursday. At the last hearing, although he consented to the adjourn- ment to Thursday, he said that he was not sure that he could attend, and promised to give intima- tion to the registrar in case he could not. This he did, but the jury, and witnesses, of which there is a great number, were not aware of this, aud were in attendance at the court. Two men, named George Wright, Lowerhouse-lane, and Alfred Beesley, Derby-road, Farnworth, were ad- mitted into the Accident Hospital on Tuesday, suffering from scalds. Both men were employed at the Atlas Works, and were working on a plank, which suddenly broke, and they were precipitated into a vitro! well be- neath. NO REASONABLE OFFER REFUSED, if made at once at Mama's Furniture Warehouse, Bridge Foot, Warrington, —See fuller Adv., page 4.—Arirl. DEATH OF MR. T. H. CLEVELAND.

Many persons in Warrington will regret to hear of the death of Mr. Thomi.s henry Cleveland,which took place at Folkestone,at the house of his daugh- ter, Mrs. F. Rogers. Mr. Cleveland at one time editor of the now defunct " Evening Post," at Warrington, and had also held a similar position in connection with the "Guardian " and Evening News," Leicester, and the " Kendal and County News." lie suffered, for some time previous to his death, from cancer in the chest, and died on Satur- day last at the age of 57 years. his remains were interred in Folkestone cemetery on Thursday after- noon.
