DEATH AND FUNERAL OF MRS. W. P. t.i KELTON.

It is with regret we announce the death of Sirs. Skelton, wife of Councillor W. P. Skelton, who died at her residence Great Sankey, at an early hour on Saturday morning, at the age of :35. She had been in a delicate state of health for some time, but though little hope was entertained of her recovery, her sudden demise caused much regret amongst a large circle of friends. The funeral took place on Tuesday afternoon, the ceremony being conducted by the Rev. E. S. Jackson, vicar of Great Sankey. The chief mourners were Councillor W. P. Skelton, Charles, Arthur, and William, sons, Mr. W. Skelton, Mr. John Skelton, and Mr. Charles Humphries. The employees of Messrs. W. P. and W. Skelton, Ltd., walked in procession to St. Mary's Church, and a good attendance of friends of the deceased were present to pay their last respects. A considerable number of floral tributes bad been sent.

THE' MERSEY EIGHTY YEARS AGO. The monthly meeting was held at the Town Hall, on Thursday. Mr. S. Taylor presided, and there were present .—Messrs. P. Speakman, IP., PROPOSED VEHICULAR BRIDGE AT C.C., R. lmison, W. Handley, F. Wiswall, J. RUNCORN. Once, J. W. Lightburn, J. Pritchard, W. Collier, W.. Hankinson, D. Bisbrown, .J. /teadowcroft, W. Leach, IL Abel, F. A. Lake (law clerk). E. Marshall (secretary), M. Barker (surveyor). , NEAL= OF um TOWN. The Medical Officer of Health (Dr. MacDougall) re- ported that the number.of deaths registered during the past month amounted to 36. Of this number 5 were due to natural decay,. 7 to pneumonia, 4 to diarrhoea, 4 to premature birth, 2 to bright's disea;e, 2 to convulsions 2 to bronchitis, snd one each to puerperal, blood poison- ing, apoplexy, diabetes, heart disease, erysipelas, coma, inflammation of windpipe, misenteric disease, scarletina and cancer. The 36 deaths showed an average death rate of 21.6 per 1,000 of the estimated population for the twelve months. Eleven of the deaths were infanta under twelve months, and 3 were under 2 years of age, and 3 were over 80 years of age.—ln reply to Mr. Light. burn, the Doctor said he considered the report very satisfactory.

A FORGOTTEN WARRINGTON PAMPHLET. We are indebted for the following highly inter- esting article to Mr. Wm. Handley, of Runcorn. For some time I had heard of a book being in existence, written more than fifty years ago, ad- vocating the building of a bridge across the Mersey at Runcorn Gap, and a few weeks ago I had the pleasure ot perusing an original copy. This copy found bound up with a number of pamphlets of local antiquarian intereat in the Liverpool Free Library, under number F 1661. It is entitled " A Letter to the Mayor of Liverr.mol, relative to a Bridge over the Mersey at Runcorn, by John Dumbell," and dated 1813. It is illustrated by a copper plate map, and issued at the price of 3s. The writer dates his pamphlet from Mersey Mills, Latchford, Warrington. Mr. Bombe)! takas up his pen in advocacy of a new and shorter way or main road to and from London and Liverpool than then existed, with two branch main roads from a point near Lower Whitley about eleven and a half miles distant from Runcorn, one branch road leading through Warrington and Pres- ton to the North and the other leading through Chester and North Wales to Bangor. Ilia proposal embraced two bridges over the Mersey, one at Latchford, where the river was at that time crossed by a ferry near How- ley Weir, and the other at Runcorn Gap, where there W. 5 a larger ferry. From the language used in the pamphlet. I should judge Mr. John Ditinbell to have been a man of sanguine temperament, to have seen WC° difficulty in overcom- ing adverse conditions and not very sparing in his esti- mate of those more cautious persons who chanced to differ from him. But as the extracts I made from the r•amptilet, and herein quoted, will reveal to some extent his plan and purpose, 1 will leave them to explain them- selves, merely remarking that his suggestions have in the main been long since accomplish( d facts, although not exactly in the way he indicated. At once thence to the quotations By the petition now before the House of Commons, you will see that a plan is offered to the legislature for establishing a fi,hery in the rivers Mersey, Irwell and Weaver, from whieh the public will derive much advan- tage by the protection of spawn and constant supply of fish, and the urofits of this extensive ty.dertaking are to be applied to build a bridge over time Mersey at Rune mrn and another at Wartington,and after,to the support of a General Infirmary near Warrington and of Chester and Liverpool Infirmaries. The situation at Runcorn is peculiarly formed by nature for a bridge by the con- traction of the width of the stream and the firm rock upon which the structure will be made. This spot, pointed out by nature as the situation for a bridge, has long been the subject of general con- templation. A Bridge at Runcorn will lead directly,and cowequently be the means of bringing the mail from London to Liverpool at six o'clock in the evening instead of twenty-five minutes past ten o'clock the following morning, and of obtaining as much time in its return ; as it may set off for London at nine o'clock in the morn- ing instead of 10 o'clock the preceding night." "A letter from London to Liverpool is thirty-six hours old before it is opened, whereas a letter from Bristol to Liverpool can i.e opened in fifteen hours. By the build- ing of a bridge at Runcorn Gap, letters can be delivered at Liverpool eight hours earlier even by the present mail coat:hos, and a bridge at this place is therefore the first step towards this great d‘-sideratum and the next step follows as .t thing of course ; the improvement of the roads to and from Runcorn." .

" Your petitioner,therefore, humbly prays this honor- able house that leave may be given to bring in a Bill for incorporating certain persons therein to be named a Fishery Company with powers to raise such a joint stock as may be requisite by loan orothervrise and to apply the same to the purposes before mentioned under such rules, and regulations and restrictions in the said Bill,as ta this honourable house shall seem meet." As a necessary addendum to the petition is set out a draft prospectus containing the following financial proposal. The capital required was to he subscribed in 2,500 shares of the nominal value of £lOO each,yielding a total of £150,000. The amount payable on each share was strictly limited to its nominal value. Toe first call of 20s. per share to be paid on January Ist, 1814, future calls to be made at a rte of not more than 20, per share the maximum rate of interest being fixed at 7i per cent. though 5 per cent, only was to be paid for the first 12 months. No repayment of capital was to be made earlier than January Ist, 1819. Copies of both petition and prospectus were to be obtained from a large number of persons, specified amongst whom appear the names of, Mr. John Whitler, solicitor, Liverpool ; Mr. Fenn, Yarn- worth ; Mr. Davies, the White flart,Runcorn ; and John Dumbell, the secretary, Mersey Mills, Warrington.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT

RUNCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

Councillor O'Donnell, of Widnes, has given notice that at the next meeting of the Free Library Com- mittee he will move that the reading room of the Free Public Library shall be opened on Sundays. Public houses are open on Sundays, and Coun- cillor O'Donnell believes that the reading room will be an inducement to keep people out of those places.

The Town Clerk of Widnes acting under the in- structions of the Library Committee, is endeavour- ing to secure a course of Gilchrist L2ctures for the borough before the winter closes.

The agenda of Widnes Town Council meeting on Tuesday next, will contain a proposition to estab- lish a Borough Bench. Congratulations to Mr. Henry Linaker, of Runcorn, who passed his inter- mediate examination, at the recent examination held under the Incorporated Law Society. Mr. Linaker is the son of the late Mr. W. H. Linaker, who for many years was law adviser to the Board of Commissioners and many other public •bodies, and he is articled to his uncle, Mr. Arthur Linaker, solicitor, High-street, Runcorn.

Runcorn Moo! Board hav3 determined to in- crease the accommodation for girls, at Greenway- road school, by constructing an extra classroom. Places will thus be found for over eighty more children. But the boy's school is crowded to over- flowing ;,what is to be done here ?

Runcorn Musical Society will Rive the second concert of the reason, in the Drill Hall, on Wednes- day next. The work for performance is Handers " Samson," and that full justice may be done to the noble composition, Runcorn and Warrington Isocieties, are joining forces on this occasion, and there will be a chorus of 200 voices and a capable orchestral band. The principals are Miss Thudich um Madame Alice Bertenshaw, Mr. J. Gawthrop and Mr. Bantock Pierpoint. With these talented artistes and a powerful chorus, led by the baton of Mr. F. H. Crossley, a performance of the highest class may be anticipated, and those of the public who do not attend will be the losers.

There died at Runcorn on Wednesday the oldest member of the Runcorn circuit of the Primitive Methodist Church in the person of Mrs. Elizabeth Stubbs, of Regent-st. She had been a member of the church for no fewer than sixty years, and up to the last took great interest in the work of the de- nomination. In the summer she laid one of the memorial stones of the new school in Greenway- road. Mrs. Stubbs was 72 years of age.

The sixth of the series of Gilchrist Lectures, secured by Runcorn Free Library Committee, will be delivered in Runcorn Public flail cn Thursday evening next. Professor 11. 0. Seeley will deal with the interesting subject of volcanoes.

THE TECHNICAL INSTITUTE FOIL WIDNES. -- ..

In the Council Chamber of Town Hall, on Thursday at noon, Col. Charles IL Limn], R.'., an inspector of the Local Government Board, held an inquiry into the application by the Widnes Town Council for sanction to borrow £9,415 for the erec- tion of a Technical Institute and Free Public Library. There were present the Mayor (Alderman Gossage), Messrs. H. Wade Deacon, C.C., F. Neil, D. J. Brookes, 13. S. Oppenheim (town clerk), and T. Craik (assistant clerk). In supporting the application, the Town Clerk said the site for the building had been gener- ously given by the trbstees of the Leigh estate and was well situated. The total amount required to put up the building was £10,415, but the Corporation had received a grant of £l,OOO from the science and art department, reducing the amount, by that sum. The Corporation were at present conducting classes in higher and second grade subjects in Major Cross•st., but the accommoda- tion was very inadequate for the purpose. The present Library premises formed part of the Town Hall and had always been regarded as temporary. There were 5,807 books in the Library, the average daily issue was 190 and the daily average attend.ince in the reading-room was 517. The borrowing powers of the Corporation under the Public Health Act were £150,000, and the sure borrowed was £78,085, allowing a margit. of £74,015. The Corporation desired that the time for repayment should be extended for a period of 50 years. Times had been very hard in the borough and the rateable value of the town was not llkly to be elastic for some time to come. There was no objection, and the Inspector hav- ing examined the plans, the inquiry terminated.

The Market Cortimittee recommended that any person selling goods auhject to market tolls shall attend at the market for the purpose of paying the toll, to the Market Superintendent before offering any article for sale. Mr. Hankinson moved and Mr. Grice seconded the confirma- tion of the minutes. It was explained th it there was no intention to stop these people hawking but they must first attend at the market and pay the toll. Comments were made upon the hardship of causing the persons to carry their baskets to the market,and thon perhaps have to retrace their steps to Higher Runcorn.—After some discussion, the minutes were referred back to the Cella- mit tee for further consideration. " That it be represented to the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners that the amount required for ;he erec; ion of a Technical Institute will in all probability be forth- csming, and that the Commissioners be strongly urged to proceed forthwith with the building of the Institute." The meeting was a very successful one. A sum of about £l2O was promised them, a fact which argued well for the successful raising of the balance required to establish the building.—iffr. Handley seconded, and re- marked that the meeting of the Commissioners put the matter on a go-a-head footing, and it only needed a con- tinuation of that feeling to make the institute a com- plete success, and raise the money by voluntary sub- scriptions of those who would receive niot benefit from it. The Commirodonen would best inspire confidence by showing what they were prepared to do themselves. If the whole of the Commissioners would state what they 'would give, the ratepayers generally would not be slow to follow cult—Mr. Light burn had no doubt the money could be raised by voluntary subscription, and he hoped that the Commissioners and those who had not thought about the matter would make up their minds, and this would have a great influence upon the ratepayers ger-lo- nely. - FINANCE. The Finance Committer rep•rrted that the receipts fur the month amounted to £444 0. 31. on general account, £l9l 14s. 51 on water account, and £1 Bs, 7d. on Library fund. The following accounts were passed for payment : general fund, £597 33. itt.; water account, £2426. 9.1. ; water loan account, £6945. 4.1.; private street works account, £551 15s. 7d. ; permanent works loan account, £35 123. 7d.—Mr. unison moved the adoption of the minutes.—Mr. J. W. Lightburn seconded, and in doing so made an important statement with res- pect to THE PORT OF RUNCORN. Ile said it would be well for the information of the Com- missioners and of the 'public that he should explain what had been going on in connection auth the changing of r the name and character of the Fort of Runcorn. Ile showed that the port commenced at the Cheshire aide at Ince, passed up the river, including Saltport, Weston Point, Runcorn and Latchford ; and on the Lancashire side from Dungeo:i Point, Hale Head, the landing-places ' at Dittrm, Widnes, Fiddler's Ferry, including Wai eng- , ton. Now in the Manchester Ship Canal Act the com- pany had powers to make the Port of Manchester include all business that WAS transacted in the canal. Ile wished it clearly to be understood that the Customs arrange- ment; were entirely in the hands of the Treasury, who had the absolute power to make arrangements with :ut consulting Runcorn at all, the town authority not bt ing in a position to say yes or no In order to support their case, a deputation went to London on November 13 and pleaded in favour of their rights On November 25 they were requested to meet the heads of the Customs de- partment, at Liverpool, to receive the decision of the Treasury. This was practically that all the Customs facilities should be retained ; that the clearing and re- porting of ships, the paying off and engaging of crews, the registration of vessels, and the bonded stores sheuld remain as before. ' They, however, could not see their way to Runcorn maintaining the name of port. The names of vessels could still appear as Mary of Runcorn, not Mary of Manchester, but the word port could Rot be inscribed. Therefore the 100 or 200 vessels would still carry the name of the town throughout the world. It was the intention to increase the Customs officers at Runcorn, so that it was evidently expected that more business would he done in that direction than before. 11e wished it to bo understood that the deputation re- ceived the greatest kindness from Mr. Williams, head of ' the Customs Department, Li verpool,who had given Run- corn every facility within his power. Runcorn would still retain all her rights and privileges, as well as an extended boundary, but she would lose the title of being a port. Mr. Lightburn was requested to present a written re- port so that it should be recorded and the minutes passed. THE PROPOSED BYELAWS. . . . _

The ChairmanZteTth-a-t-a: letter had been received from the contractors of the town asking the committee to receive a deputation as they objected to certain of the new building beelaws, and desired to point out the reasons. The conference WAS held on the previous day. The deputation pointed out a number of grievances they had respecting the byelaws. but the Commissionnrs present gave them to understand that the Local Gov- ernment Board would mot make any alteration in them. The Board had had only two courses open, namely, adopt them as they were, or drop. Their only remedy would be to apply to the Local Government Board. The deputation left with that understanding. Mr. Imison considered that under the excumstances the most effective way of discussing the subject was to requt st the Byelact s Committee to prepare a report of the conference which took place, and that it should con- tain a digest of the objections raised. He moved that Mich a course be adopted. Mr. Meadoweroft seconded the proposition. If the contractors had grievances they fihnuld ke submitted to the Board, and if the members were satisfied with the bylaws :lc they stood, then they should proceed with them. THINGS HEARD AND SEEN

AT WARRINGTON.

The poor juryman has had another example of the inexorable calls of justice at the IVarrington County Court, on Thursday, and truth to say, ex- pressions of annoyance used on that occasion were not without foundation. The case over which they sat in judgment came up for hearing about a month ago, and took the greater part of the day,and when the time for rising arrived, it had not been half heard, and it had accordingly to be adjourned until last Thursday. Mr. Mulholland, the oarriater en- gaged in the case for the prosecution was not quite certain that he could attend, but promised to send an intimation to the registrar in case he could not. This he appears to have done on Wednesday, but the jury were net informed, and after wasting a whole day a month ago, they wasted a morning I again on Thursday, and Were then told that the case would be adjourned for another week. The jury were naturally rather " riled "at this, and Mr. Brown, as their spokesman, gave utterance to their annoyance, but he was "imply told that unless the judge ordered otherwise, he would have to come, Or be under a penalty.

This is rather too hard upen the jury. Here were five business men who had already spent a day mad a half with the prospect of another day in ft court which is the reverse of comfortable. The time of five jurymen is surely as precious as that oh one barrister, be be ever so eminent,and should be taken into account, because all the jury get, are the thanks of the judge which do not go very far to reimburse them for the time they have lost Everything con- sidered, I sympathise most strongly with those five poor jurytnen.

One cause or another seems to be having an effect upon meetings in Warrington just now. The usual gathering of the Warringten Debating So- ciety should have been held in Sr. Atitin's Cham- bers on Tuesday evening,but at the time for a com- mencement only two membets had put in an ap- pearance. As matters did not improve much as time went on, the officers being ab-ent through sickness, it was finally decided to abandon tho meeting, and the essayist departed with his undelivered paper.

" Before a bridge is built at Runcorn no raedsal system of improvement can be made in the road to and from Liverpoql and London, but when this bridge is agreed upon, we get two fixed objects, a point to set out from and a point to aim at; these two points would naturally be the Dome of St. Petits hnd the Liverpool Town Hall, but the river Mersey interposes in such a way as to dictate that the Castle Rock of Rencern must be substituted for the Livereeol Town Hall and not ()illy the works of nature, but these of art are peculiarly favourable for drawing a straight line from London to Runcorn in preference. to any other place as such a line would fall over a large rirtion c f the present Mail Coach road to Liverpool, viz., through Stony Stratford, 'Few- rester, Daventree, Ounchurch, and Newcastle-under- Lyme ; it consequently follows that a bridge at Runcorn offers the greatest advantages of leading not only the attention of mankind at present but that of future ages." Mr. Dumbell then gees on to compare the proposed new road as a kind of Roman road, urges Liverpool, throueh its chief magistrate, to emulate the ancient Romans by _undertaking the initiative in the grand work, lie points out that the then mail coach road to Londe was 206 miles long, that his proposal would reduce this by 27 miles, thus: Liverpool to Runcorn, 113/4 miles ; Runcorn to Junction Point, near Lower Whitley, 113/4 miles ; from Junction Point to London, 156 miles ; total, 179 ; and that this saving of 27 miles would reduce the time of convevine the malls by the present coaches by neslit hours af their average rate of speed. ele,however, advocated their abolition, to a great extent, for expresd delivery, as he would establish a system of carrying the mails on hersebeckewhich he maintained would be from their greater speed a protection from robbery and a train- ing for t lie cavalry. His plan consisted in the mails be- ing. carried by 13 relays of horsemen in the first 158 miles, that they would thus change every hour after covering the distance by a hand gallop of twelve miles an hour ; that from the junction point other relays of horsemen should be in waiting to take and convey the mails to Warrineten, Freston, and the north on the one hand, Chester and Bangor on the other ; to Runcorn on the direct route to Liverpool, and thence by the last change to Liverpool itself. The mail by this scheme • I would be delivered in fifteen hours. The exact plan on which he proposed to build the Bridge, Mr Dumbell does not positively indicate, but after describing the pier bridges of the Theines and other rivers, he unhesitatingly expresses his belief that a pler bridge would be both unsuitable and dangerous if placed across Runcorn Gap and gives in support of his opinion : ' The width of the river at Runcorn Gap at high water is 412 yards and 294 yards at low water, the deep being wholly on the Cheshire aide, with a shelving rock on the Lancashire shore. The tide runs with such iii. peuosity throueli the gap that I shall be afraid that the Vessels could not obey their rudders if the bridge were constructed with piers." Ile concludes his pamphlet with the prediction " That a bridge will be built at Runs corn r cannot entertain the most remote doubt, the ac- cent enclosure of Delamere Forest, is now alone sufficient .demand one, nothing can prevent a bridge being built at Runcorn but the decay of commerce." As a necessary appendix he gives the text of the peti- tion which of itself possesses great interest as showing another aspect of the Mersey than can well he belietrad or understood by the present generation, on this ac- count, I proceed to quote it : " The humble petition of John Dumtell of Warrington in the county of Lancaster, gent. That during the time In which he enj iced the same, the fish mar kets of the several towns and places near the banks of the river were abundantly supplied with fish, and in particular with salmon and spurlings. The formation of a Sunday School Union for the banding together of the various Nonconformist Sunday schools in the town, may be eaid to supply a long felt want. The objects to be derived from such a Union are becoming more and more necessary as the religious education of children is now almost entirely left to the sunday school. The present is a day of unions, and the Sunday Schools by thus banding together will enable the strong to help the weak ; methods of management may be discussed, and visitation of schools arranged. Additional benefits will also be received from the London Sunday School Union in the furnishing of Sunday Schools, and in providing li.erature at more ad- vantageous terms than was before possible.

From the statement of Councillor Shaw Cireen at the Council meeting in respect to t he recent examin- ation for labour certificates, it would seem that the examination has been a success from a numerical point of view. But there are many pungent ques- tions which might be asked as to the wisdom of providing local employers of labour with these boy labourers at the age of 12 or 13 years, who are now placed in the labour market. The object presunt- ably is that the boys may thus earn Sonic+ portion of their daily bread, and thereby relieve their parents, a little earlier than was before possible. • But the movement appears to be a step backwards. Boys who leave school at such an age, are deprived of that technical, and perhaps most, essential educa- tion, which is given in the higher standards of elementary schools. They are thus weakened in the battle of life, for to neglect education in the present age is a suicidal policy on the part of parents. It may perhaps he Anted that there are the continuation and technical schools, but atten- dance at these is optional,and really only a tithe of the elementary school scholars take advantage of them,

The case heard in the Police Court on Monday, in which three publicans were summoned and tined for serving policemen on duty, omdirms what has been freely rumoured of late. that this practice has been going on pretty regularly in the town. Now that it has been brought to the light of day,and the licenk of one public-liouse has been endorsed in consequence, it will no doubt act as a check upon the practice for a considerable time.

Councillor Percy Davies made his fledid in the Town Council on Tuesday night, and he made it in quite a notable fashion. Lie had not been on his feet very long before he trod upon the corns, or. to put it lees figuratively, he soon roused the ire of some of the worthy aldermen and councillors. Coun- cillor Davies is something like the livelylrishmen at Donnybrook fair,—" When he sees a head he will bit it." He, however, did it very good naturedly, and also received the blows in return in a similar spirit. Ile had tried to" behave himself," he said, but had failed. Without wishing to unduly dis- parage Mr. Ashton, I may aay that, to my way of thinking, Mr. Davies is an improvement upon his predecessor in the Latchford ward, and be will not be quite such a docile follower of the dominant party in the Town Ceuncil.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATE.

TRAVELLING WITHOUT A TICKET. William Connor, fish dealer,was charged at the War- rington Borough Court, on Saturday, with travelling on the Leindon and North-Western Railway frii.m Eatles- town to Warrington without a ticket, and also with assaulting Detective Ede whilst in the exPcution of his duty. Detective Ede stated that about 855 the previous night he heard an altercation going on with the defend- ant and the ticket collector. tie (witness) asked for an explanation from the prisoner, and he said he hadn't come by train, and refused to pay any fare. He was told he would have tc be locked up, and he then offered to pay the fare from Earlestown,but it was refused. He was taken to the booking office, and the prisoner threw witness to the ground, and on the way to the Police Station the prisoner commenced kicking and used very bad language. Witness blew his whistle and a police constable came to his assistance. Ticket collector Hol- land corroborated. P.C. Clark said about 9.20 he was in Wilson Patten-st., when he heard a whistle and on going in that direction he saw the prisoner and Detective Ede on the ground.—The magistrates fined prisoner 20s. and costs, or 14 days. He went to gaol.

_ CRUELTY TO HORSES. Sidney Ashton and William Spencer, both of Stockton Heath, were summoned before the Warrington magis- trates, yesterday, the farmer charged with working a horse_while in an unfit condition, and the latter with causing it to be so worked, on November 29. Inspector Luff, It S.P.C.A., said that he met tiae defendant Ash- ton driving the horse attached to a coal cart, in Bridge- street, on the above date. He noticed that it " flinched" very much under the saddle, and upon ex- amining the animal's back, he found a wound, from which blood and matter was oozing. A blanket had been placed over the wounded part, and it was quite wet. Upon Spencer being informed, he said that the horse had only a small wound of the size of a three- penny ' piece, when it set oat.—The magistrates fined Ashton in the costs, and Spencer 10s. and costs. John Brecklehurst, Antrobos, Cheshire, was charged with travelling a horse while in an unfit state, on Nov. 30th.—Inspector Luekings, R.S.P.C.A., said that on the day in question, which was horse fair, witness was going about the fair, when ha saw prisoner walking a horse about. It appeared so latne, that witness stopped him, and found that the horse was suffering from a cankered foot, which was in a spongy and diseased state. Tors wes no shoe upon the fcot, which was too muhh diseased to hold a nail. The horse was sold to a horse dealer subsequently, who took it to a tanyard.— Abel Wright said that he considered that defendant had committed an abominable piece of cruelty and he would be fined 20s. and costs, or 14 days' hard labour in de- fault. - -

TEEFT FROM THE MARKET HALL. At the Borough Court on Thursday, Elizsbeth Long- shaw,a married woman,living at 12,Angel Yard,off Bill- et., was charged with stealing from the Market Pt ice, on Saturday, the 2nd December, a quantity of sweets, value 55., the property of Messrs. Naylor and Watson, confectioners, also a fur tippet, value 10s., the property of Rachel Tasker, stall holder in the market, and also on the 4th November, a man's coat and vest, the property • of Morris Goldstone, from his stall in the market. Pri- soner pleaded not guilty. Each charge was taken separ- ately. The Chief Constable stated with regard to the theft of sweets, that D.S. Monaghan from information received went to the house of the prisoner, and found a box containing a large quantity of sweets there. When asked where she got them from she replied that she bought them from a man for sixpence oa saturday night. The box was taken from Messrs. Naylor' s stall in the market between ten and eleven o'clock on Saturday night. Isabella Naylor, and DS. Monaghan co. roborated.— The Chief Constable said the complainant in the second charge was Rachael Tasker, residing at 8, Rose-st. D.S. Monaghan discovered the tippet produced hanging on the line in the house, and when asked about it the pri- soner said she had it sent to I,er from Wales. Mrs. Tasker identified it as her property. Mary Fisher gave evidence for the prosecution .—The Chief Constable said that the coat and vest in the third charge were missed at seven o'clock cn Saturday night, the 4th November. by the complainant Morris Goldstone, stall hol&r, and he gave information to the police. The pawnbrokers were supplied with a sample of the missing clothes, and on Monday the 6th November, the prisoner offered them in pledge at Mr. Morton's pawnshop, Winwick-St. Jaseph Hill, shop-assistant, noticed the clothing, and went out for a policeman, but the prisoner ran away and left the coat and vest behind. The police were apprised, and Hill now identified the prisoner as the woman who offered the clothes in pledge. The clothes were found to be the property ef the complainant. Evidence in sup- port of the Chief Constable's statement was given by Messrs. Guldstone and Hill, and D.S. Monaghan. The magistrates committed the prisoner to jail for one month on each charge, three months in all.

COSTUME ON THE STAGE.

One great difficulty and annoyance to me is the unwillingness of many inartistic actresses to wear any- thing that they imagine is unbecoming to them, and the carelessness of " supers" and chorus-women in the matter of wearing a dress correctly. The prevailing fashion always holds absolute sway over feminine minds, and they will always contrive ta intrAduce a suggestion of it—either by altering the shape of a wig, the fuloess and position of a sleeve, or generally trying to modernise their appearance. Yet the stage sometimes has an appreciable influence on the fashions of real life. That particular kind of fashion called the " accordion pleat" was shown to the milliners, and they with unanimity, refused to have anything to do with it. I intro- duced it into the dresses of the dancers in the " Paste-de-Quatre " in " Faust-UP-to-Date" at the Gaiety, and afterwards designed a white peignoir in the same material for Miss Lefty Lind in " Buy Bias." Im- mediately one saw it on every side, and the" accordion plat" soon became as vulgar and objectionable as the barbaric instrument of music from which it derived its name. And when Miss Ellen Terry appeared, as Lady Macbeth, with a gown embroidered with Indian beetles, which made a splendid foil to the auburn tresses of her hair, many ladies of fashion appeared with their dresses jauntily decorated with the Iridescent insect in question. Percy Anderson, in " Magazine of Art."

Mr. Alexander Peckover, the newly-appointed Lord- Lieutenant ef Cambridgeshire, is the first Nonconformist who has ever held such an office. Mr. Peckover is an extremely wealthy bank'-,is a member of the Society of Friends, and belongs t a family that is famous for its philanthropy throughout the whole county. La politics he is an ardent Liberal, and it is in no small degree ow- ing to his support that the Finn Arthur Brand was able to win and hold North Cambrid4.•—•.lre for the Liberals against the couple of fierce on•lsu,eits which Mr. Surr W.Duncan, of Horsforth, made ~n H:c• constituency. The Medical Annual advises Prset.tioners to remember that when recommending Cocoas as )(NA and beverage for Invalids, the name CADBURY on any packet of Cocoa is a gusranteeoi purity.—Adrt. Dr. Conan Doyle is in the black books of the citizens of Edinburgh, says the "London Scotsman." It was advertised thsit he would deliver ' a lecture on " Facts about Fiction," and the announcement attracted a vast audience. Great was their disappointment when the creator of the British Vidocq intimated that someone ' had blundered, and that he was to speak on "George Meredith ''— a subject of only •limited interest to the many.

.. RUGBY. WARRINGTON V. ROCHDALE HORNETS.

That person of chilly manners, " Jack Frost," held the field on the Causeway in his hoary grip, on Saturday. Consequently the ground possessed much of what is termed " bone." Footballers realize what this means best, when they are roughly tackled. There was" bone" of another character introduced into the game, how- , ever. It was the famous " bone of contention." After the ground was known to be in such a frost bitten con- dition the Hornets were somewhat loath about carrying out the fixture, but after a little friendly debate it was agreed that the fixture should be played off as a League rnateh. In the meantime, while the teams were " gird- ing on their armour," an army of 300 youths were turned upon the ground with a number of footballs. After a little friendly rivalry—some people described it otherwise—between those 300 youthful enthusiasts, the ground began to lose much of its hard nature. Despite this however, it was thought that some special restric- tions concerning the tackling would have been fixed. This was not done, but instead, the referee followed the tackling up very closely, and the promptness of his de- cisions was very commendable. Both teams were very representative ones. Their composition upon assemb- ling to the shrill piping of the referee's whistle was thus;— Warrington; Heesom, back ; Carey, Evans, Potts.and Massey, three-quarter backs ; James Bate and John Bate (captain), half backs; Fair. Turner, J. Jolley, Berry, Donohue, Thorniley, Bailey, Taylor, and Buck- ley , forwards. Rochdale Hornets: Smith, back ; Holds. worth, W. Ogden, ‘Vightman, and Isherwood, three- quarter backs ; J Ogden and Flowarth, halfbacks; Der, Butterworth, Horsfield, Leach. Crabtree, Simpson, Ashworth, and Kershaw, forwards. Referee, Mr. S. The Insects set off with considerable dash, and gave people the impression that they were going to sting, for a time. Heesom, however,managed to relieve the pres- sure, and then Warrington get back to their own quar- ters by the introduction of some smart combination. John Bate cleverly gained possession at the Warrington 25, and passed to " Smack " who transferred to Carey. The latter was joined by Potts, and the two I romped down the field, passing and repaasing to each other in a manner which simply made the spectators cheer. The sequel to this smart performance, was, on the contrary, quite cheerless. Just when play had been brought within range of the Rochdale line, Evans was given a chance to provide some work for the scorer. But instead of working for the try, which was certainly obtainable, he kicked. All the advantage that had bees gained was soon lost, for the Insects made a desperate dash and soon conveyed play to the other end of the ground. Here their forwards were beginning to assume a dangerous attitude, and were giving the gentleman who marks the scores cause to prepare for some occupa- tion. But at a critical point, Heesom saved by kicking a ball dead. The drop out, however, brought no ease to the home team, for Ashworth received and made a dodgy run through a crowd of home men, and made his way to within two yards of the geed line. At this point he was" cornered," and his pass came too late to beef any service. The visiting forwards were playing des- perately hard, and their exploits were often only checked by the tact of the local backs. Play continued to go on in a fast, though scientific manner, until quite near to the ' interval. Then the Warrington backs by a little careful combination sent the ball down upon the visitors. Isher- wood, about the smartest of their three-quarters, re- turned the kick and enabled Taylor to place his capa- cious paw upon the oval. Having gained possession, Taylor made a race for the goal line. Be had traversed halt the length of the field when Ashworth came racine along at his heels. The excitement for a moment War. intense ; the air was rent with cries of "go it Taylor," and when at length the individual scored at the corner the cries of encouragement gave way to a loud cheer. Potts, however, failed to convert. No other scoring was done before the interval. On resuming Potts soon obliged with a good run, and-it was now Warrington's turn to attack. The forwards worked the scrimmages excellently, and several fine rushes along the popular touch were productive of an almost permanent attack James Bate tricked finely, and Taylor and Turner ran well, but the effort lacked finish. Wightman ran the oval out to near half-way, but a splendid dribble by Buckley was the means of Warrington renewing the attack. Holdsworth saved well, and passing by James and John Bate almost resulted in something substantial. The visitors were hard pressed, and but for the invalu- able defence of Ogden and Howarth, the locals must have scored. Simpson led the way to the central line, but another passing bout by Taylor, Carey, and Potts, was spoiled by the accurate tackling of Wightman Scrimmages ensued on the Hornets' goal line, and from one of these the ball passedthrough a considerakle num- ber of hands, Massey eventually dropping at goal with a barren result. Soon after the drop-out another fine passing movement by John and James Bate, Evans and Massey saw the locals again pressing, and Donohue next gained forty yards in response to a kick by Isherwood. Carey twice over effected capital runs with no success, whilst Taylor gained what was apparently a fair try be- tween the posts. Rochdale had one splendid opening ten miuntes from the call uf time, but too many hands spoiled the chance, and from this until the close the Warringtonians kept up the attack, but with no success and a grand game ended with Warrington winners by a try (three points). WaRRINGTON V. LANSDOWNS.—Despite the inclemency of the weather, there was a capital attendance at Wil- derspool on Monday to witness this hastily arranged match. The Irishmen are on tour, and had already played St. Helens Recreation a good game on Saturday. The kick off was not responded to, and Massey eventually gave his side a footing past the half way flag. Passing by the Bates ended in Massey still improving the locals' position, and Carey with a neat kick tackled Woodeide before he could respond. Capital rushing by the burly Irishmen resulted in half way, but Berry and Buckley headed the home side to within a few yards of the Lansdowne goal line Heesom and Anderson exchanged with advantage to the first named, and then came free kicks to each side, but a round of passing by the Bates, Carey, Potts, and Bur- ton saw the latter grassed on the line. Lansdowne were penalised, but the kick at goal struck the upright, and Thorniley raced up for a try, which Potts failed to im- prove upon. The locals came very near scoring a minute later, for a tush by Berry and Buckley Saw Woodside

W&RRINGTON 11. Sr. ELPHIN'S—This match was played on Saturday. The ground, though hard, was in good condition, and the weather was fine. At three o'clock both teams took their positions on the field as fellows : Warrington: Goal, Forrest; full backs, Clarke and J. Mather; halves, Linaker, Williamson and Anthony; for- wards, Read, Norman, Connor, T. Mather and Fennelly. St. Elphin's : Goal, Jones ; full backs, Marshall and the Rev. T. W. Cook; halves, Colley, Forbes and Sherman; forwards, Radford, Higginbotham, Tickle, Taylor and flaVon. The game was an excellent one in many ways and the manner in which the Warrington men stuck to heir work entitled them to victory. For Warrington. Forrest was quite safe as custodian, the full backs both played capitally, Williamson was the pick of three good halves, and the forwards played an excellent combined and vigorous game. For St. Elphin's Jones, in goal, acquitted himself well, Gook, assisted by Marshall, played a brilliant game, Forbes was the best half, but he forwards played a disappointing game, and did not -haw up as they might have done. The final result was Warrington four goals, St. glphin's one.

From the restart e :erything at first went in favour of the school, and tle Crewe goal was bombarded, but good goalkeeping and a good amount of luck saved them from a severe de- feat, the upr7ghts and crossbar being hit several time, nd only one point was added to the Penketh score. Pollard at half-back played a 4plendid game, feeding his forwards with a great mo un t of judgment, and doing a great amount of hard work.

"THAT BROKKEN ARM !"

Tune---" Willie. we bye missed you." Ey, Tommy, we hev missed thi, Evity yUre o' thi yed, Aw cudn't boss to tell thi _ _

Heawm'ny tears hez bin shed. We cum an' go i' trubbie, But aw thi owd mates jine wish in' theau wur better, Wi' that brokken arm o' thine ! Folks axes," Wheer's Tommy ?" An' echo answers " Wheer ?" Ther griet's too hard fer howdin', Wen they see that vacant cheer. Its bitten um so keenly, Thay dun nowt else but pine, An' caunt contain ther sorrow O'er that brokken arm o' thine. Thoose New Lone chaps that's famous Fer raisin' up Chrysants, Are gettin' daily smawer Abeaut ther bally-bants. It gains noan wot they're drinkin', Er wen thay sup er dine; They booath own they're wastin', Throo that brokken arm o' thine! We try to rom sum cumfort I' dear owd Johnson's ribs; But wen we wud console him, By Gyow ! he fairly jibs 1 He feels ez if he'd getten Cowd crills aw deawn his spine, An' feats ne'er recover That brokken arm o' thine Eawr sale o' good owd "Irish" Ez gone deawn wi' a flop; An' th' treasurer keeps swearin' He'll hey to shut up th' shop. An' then, no moor i' flookin', He'll hey a chance to shine So here's a pretty prospect, Throo that brokken arm of thine. Sum axes heaw theau did it, An' sum are quite cock sure, Theau coom agen a curb stone, That riz hauf way fro th' floor. We heerd a Black Horse purr'd thi Sumwheer on th' Wiggin line; An' that wuz heaw theau geet it— That brokken arm o' thine! But ne'er mind heaw theau did it, Cum back ez soon ez t' con; Befoor eaur hopes are blighted, An' aw eawr pluck ez gone. We miss thi, 0 we miss thi, An' it needs no words o' mine, To prove its play'd the divil— That brokken arm o' thine! Rose Bonk, Dec. 8, 1893.

SOAP

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

MONDAY.

In the House of Co—mi-n On-s—o- Monday evening Mr. flaws, answering a question by Colonel Noters respect- ing allotments for Irish labourers, said the hope of the Government was that in the course of time the same facilities would be given in Ireland as in England. Sir W. HARCOURT informed Mr. Howell that be had decided to appoint a departmental committee with a view to removing any impediments to the practice adopted by the Amalgamated Society of Carpenters and Joiners of depositing their funds without limit, as pro- vident societies, in the savings hank. Mr. BUXTON, replying to Mr. Labouchere, said that if as a result of the defeat of the Matabele, and the break- ing up of their military system it proved inevitable to assume an active exercise of jurisdiction within Mata- beleland proper,as heretofore in Mashonaland, the Order in Council and the South Africa Company's charter pro- vided the necessary machinery. Mr. BRYCN having in answer to a question statod hoss far arrangements had been completed for bringing into operation the compulsory attendance provisions of the Irish Education Act of 11192, Mr. SEXTON announced that unless arrangements were made by which efficient ele- mentary schools in Ireland willing to accept the eon- science clause were admitted to a fair proportion of State aid,no forth legislation on the subject could be treated by the Irish members as non-contentious. The House again went into Committee on the Local Government (Parish Conncils) Bill. Mr. T. H. BOLTON moved an amendment that the expenses of the parish council should be defrayed out of a special rate, and that the system of compounding in reference to this rate should be abolished. The amendment was strongly op- posed by the Government, and was rejected by 191 to 132. Clauses 10 and 11 were added to the bill ,and there was some discussion on clause 12. TUESDAY. In the House of Commons on Tuesday evening Mr. GOURLEY put several questions to the Secretary for War respecting the condition of British naval stations in the Mediterranean, which led Mr. Campbell-Bannerman to remark that public mischief might be done even by put- ting such questions on the paper. The Government, he added, was not, and no Administration could be, Indifferent to the important duty of maintaining the de- fences of Malta and Gibraltar. Answers to questions given by the Post-master General and the Secretary to the Treasury indicated a willingness on the part of the Government to transfer to the district councils the nomination to post-masterships as regards the smaller post offices in the country. Mr. MUNDELLA, in replying to a question, said the coal exported during the four months August to November inclusive, amounted to 12,087,000 tons, against 14,397,000 tons in the corresponding four months of 1892. Mr. GLADSTONE, in reply to Mr. Cramer, said the differ- ent departments of the Government had a sincere desire to reduce the hours of labour to eight per day, but he could not comply with the demand that this should be done in all departments alike and at once. Mr. G. Rus- sell informed Mr. Caine that Her Majesty had approved the appointment of an Indian gentlemen to the vacant judgeship in the High Court of the North-West Provinces. The House again went into Committee on the Local Government (Parish Councils) Bill. On clause 13 Mr. STRACHEY moved an amendment to make the parish . .

council the sole trustee of all parochial charities. Mr• FOWLER opposed the amendment, declaring that the government had pledged themselves to a directly contrary course. The Solicitor General having stated that the Government were prepared to accept the prin- ciple of a later amendment providing that in certain Sages the representative element of the trustees should predominate, Mr. Gosciami said it was clear that the president of the Local Government Board had been let ' down by his colleagues, Mr. GLADSTONE said the de- claration of the Solicitor General was quite compatible with the pledges of the Government. The amendment was defeated by 251 to 67. The debate was continued till midnight.

===== 0002601_18931209_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
WEDNESDAY.

In the House of Csmmons on Wednesday discussion was resumed in committee on the Local Government (Parish Councils) Bill upon Mr. Tomlinson's amendment to clause 13, providing that when the churchwardens were trustees of a non-ecclesiastical charity the parish council might appoint a councillor and the incumbent a churchwarden to be trustees in the place of the church- wardens. Sir W. HARCOURT, in opposing the amend- ment; said that in his opinion it was an unfortunate position for the Church to claim any exclusive right to , dispose of that which was the common property of all. rue amendment was negatived by 150 to 95. Mr. Courtney moved an amendment to restrict the provis- ions of the clause in regard to the appointment of trustees in place of churchwardens to charity estates leased before the year 1840. Mr. GLADSTONE said he could not accept the amendment, which on a division was rejected by 183 to 138.

===== 0002601_18931209_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
THURSDAY

In the House of Commons on Thursday evening, Sir E. GREY, being asked if the Government intended to ap- point a commercial attache to watch over the interes ts of our trade in the United States, said the Government had no intention at present of asking Parliament to sanc- tion the expenditure necessary to provide for the ap- pointment ot any new commercial attaches. Mr. ASQUITH informed Mr. DARLING that the Govern- ment did not intend to take proceedings against the publisher of the " Commonweal " or the author of the article in that paper entitled " Bombs "as they thought that in the present state of the case such a prosecution would do more harm than good. The House again went into Committee on the Local Government (Parish Councils) Bill. On clause 13, which relates to the transference of charities, Sir F. S. POWELL moved an amendment exempting from the bill chari- ties founded after the passing thereof. Mr. FOWLER held that the amendment was entirely unnecessary, and Sir W. HARCOURT said the Government did not intend to interfere with future trusts where the creator of the rust desired that it should be kept apart from the opera- tion ot the bill. After a heated discussion the amend- ment was rejected by 207 to 171. Mr. Conn moved an amendment the object of which was to give a non- ecclesiastcial parochial charity a majority of elective trustees, and on this amendment Mr. FOWLER made a statement in which he defended himself and the Gov- ernment from the charge that they had departed from their pledges to the Church of England with regard to parochial charities. Mr. GLarisrcesfe supported Mr. Fowler's statement. The amendment was under discus- sion when midnight arrived.

The deaf juror who recurs with every assize has just appeared in an acute, and obtuse form at a town on I the south-ea‘tern circuit. He rose in the, box and asked leave to be excused on the ground of his infirmity. "Are you very deaf ?" said Lord Coleridge, almost inaudibly. " Yes, my lord, I am," wa',s the prompt reply. " Then I think you may be sworn," was the equally prompt re- joinder. A speaker at the Scottish Church Society Couferenca in Glasgow last week, advocated the unorthodox doc- trine of prayer for the dead—not prayer for all the dead, but prayer for the "blessed dead "—and by so doing rather startled some of his auditors.

TUSI 0' PEGS,

A HERO WITHOUT KNOWING IT.

Some time ago a New York policeman was alarmed by as eries of shrieks while going his rounds about two o'clock in the morning. He traced them to a parrot in a cage near the window of an hotel, in the basement of which a fire was raging fiercely. With the utmost diffi- culty he succeeded in arousing and rescuing all the inmates save a servant, who seemed, poor girl, to have lost her way in the lobbies of the hotel. Unfortunately, by the time the human beings had been saved it was too late to reach the room in which the parrot was, and the bird, but for whose persistent screams the whole of the occupants of the house would have perished, was devoured by the flames.—" Little Folks.

===== 0002601_18931209_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
SAVED BY AN UMBRELLA.

When Charles Williams, a now forgotten rhymer of Kentmere, in the Westmorland lake district, was cros- sing the hills one night, he was surprised to hear some cries for help. Running in the direction whence they came, he saw a pretty young maiden chased by a bull. Snatching her umbrella out of her hand, the poet had the presence of mind to open it in the face of the en- raged animal. This act brought the mad thing to a standatill,and it fortunately gave up the hunt. Williams saw the girl home, and was greatly charmed with her beauty and manner. A few years later they were to have been married, but the lady died of consumption, and her fate so preyed upon Williams that he put an end to his iife.—" Little Folks."

Dr. Dale, who, after a long illness, has recently re- sumed duty at Carr's-lane Chapel, Birmingham, has been for about thirty years a hard-working minister there. A tribute to his popularity appeared in a singular way the other day. A London journalist travelling on a High- land coach happened to have a young lady for a com- panion writhe box-seat. They had never met before. The conversation turned on the respective qualities of popular ministers, the gentleman at length saying, "I'll tell you what it is—Dale, of Birmingham, is the ablest man in the Congregational body." "That is my father," said the young lady.

The most popular of our ministerial writers, not ex- cepting even Canon Knox-Little or the Bishop of Ripon, is Mr. Silas K. Hocking (he has dropped the title Rever- end). The sale of his books can safely be put at eighty thousand volumes per year. He was driven into author- ship by a shower of rain which kept him indoors on one occasion when he was a minister at Burnley. To while away time, he scribbled on scraps of paper some curious stories he had heard from a seafaring uncle. He got in- terested in his work, and wrote twenty chapters. These were shown to a local editor, who offered to print them, and so appeared Mr. Hocking's first novel, "Alec Green."

EARLESTOWN AND DISTRICT HOMING

SOCIETY.

The annual general meeting of this sotiety took place on Saturday evening, at the club house, Odd- fellows' Arms, Earlestown. About 60 members; fanciers, and friends sat down to an excellently served repast,which was thoroughly enjoyed. After the cloth had neen removed, Mr. H. J. Longton, pre- sident, occupied the chair, supported by the follow- ing honorary members and visitors :—Messrs. IL Slack and A. Fletcher (Liverpool), Timperley (Stockton Heath), James Jolley and A. Twist (St. Helens), T. Harris (Cheater), W. Longton (Newton), and others. The vice chairs were filled by Dr. Patterson and Mr. S.Swann. The usual loyal toasts having been proposed and duly honoured,. the Sec- retary, Mr. J. Wightman, submitted his report for the season's 1893 work as follows :—" I have pleasure in presenting the following report of the past season.i The club has been composed of 3 honorary members and 26 flying members,as against 14 honorary members and 22 flying members in 1892. In 1891, which was the club's first season, there were 4 honorary members and 13 flying members. The reason there is such a decrease in the honorary members this season is because they gave so freely to the local relief funds, but Jam sure you will be pleased to see the club striding forward in the way of flying members. This year 1,026 birds have com- peted in the races as against 341 last year, and 135 in 1891, which goes to show that good birds are becoming numeroi,s,as well as fanciers. There have been three races in England and France for old birds, which resulted as follow,as regards the first six ie each rare —Worcester:: IR. Bartlett, 2R. Bartlett, 3 W. Jump, 4 and 5 J. Appleton, 6J. Smith. Swindon : 1 and special prize, R. Bartlett, 2J. Smith, 3 H. Hibbert, 4 EL Hibbert, 5 and 6 W. Jump. Bournemouth : 1 and sprcial, S. Dale, 2 T. Lythgoe, 3 H. J. Langton, 4 R. Bartlett, SG. Smith, 6T. Lythgoe. Cherbourg: 1 and special, W. Jump, 2 W. Jump, 3 R. Bartlett, 4 R. Bartlett, 5 S. Swann, 6H. J. Langton. There were three races for young birds, bred in 1893. which resulted as follow : Stafford: 1 and special, J. Appleton, 2 EL J. Longton, 3 J. Wightman 4E. Thelwell, 5 W. Bate, 6 J. Smith. Worcester: Ist and gold medal, J. Wightman, 2 S. Swann, 3 Ashcroft and Ball, 4 11. J. Longton, 5 W. Jump, 6W. Cheeseboro. Cheltenham: 1 and special, E. Thelwell, 2 and special, Jos. Earlam, 3 I'. Hall, 4 H. J. Longton, 5 11. J. Longton, 6 W. Jump. There was also a race for birds of any age from Gloucester on the 12th August, which resulted as follows :—Gloucester : Ist H. J. Longton, 2 T. Lythgoe, 3 W. Eaton, 4 S. Dale, SJ. Smith, 6H. S. Longton. On the 19th August, we flew a race from Swindon for aay aged birds, open to all West Lancashire, which was a great success, 91 fanciers competing, resulting as follows :-Ist silver cup, value £5 55., G. Barrow, Newton, 2 Thomas Hall, Earlestown, 3 R. Bartlett, Newton, 4 E. Burrows, Warrington, 5 T. G. Grounds, Warrington, 6 Win. Forster, Warrington, 7 T. Lytbgoe, 8 H. J. Longtnn 9 Thos. Southern (Eeriest- town), 10 Wm. Forster, 11 E. Burrows (Warrington), 12 Wm. Lealgton (Earlestown), 13 Wm. Bennett (Layton), 14 E. Burrows (Warrington), 151 J. J. Longton •(Earles- town), 16 James Lowe (Lowton), 17 James Ince (Ash- 'ton). Prizes amounting to £l9 15s. 3d. Were given in this race, among the first 6 birds, the next 11 receiving 'diplomas. As regards finances, we started the year with a balance in hand of £1 54. 6,1., and finiAred with a total profit on the year's work of £ll 19s. 81. The first prize in each race was 155., 2nd 7s. 61., 3rd 3s. 9d. He ex- pressi cl his thanks to the special prize givers for their gem rositv, and acknowl6dged the valuable assistance he had received from the president. (Applause.) Mr. Timperley afterwards handed round his world's champion from Nantes. The bird was a handsome black cock, slightly ticked white, with two or three white flights, which was much admired. He also showed the brother to the champion, which won first in the Federation race from Arvanc-hes ; this was also a very dark check. The toast of the "Visitors "given by Mr. H. J. Longton was responded to in turn by the visitors present. Letters of apology were read from several gentlemen, and the special prizes, prize money and diplomas were after- wards distributed by the President. Songs were given during the evening and other toasts were honoured, and the proceedings which had been most enthusiastic were brought to a close.

ST. HELENS TOWN COUNCIL.

-- - The monthly meeting of the St. Helens Town Council was held on Wednesday, there being thirty- two members present. Before the ordinary busi- ness was proceeded with Colonel Gamble, C. 8., referred to the illness of the Mayor (Mr. Sinclair), and read a telegram from Waterdale stating that the Mayor was still confined to his bedroom' but was feeling considerably better. (Applause.) Upon the proposition of Colonel Gamble, the Deputy - Mayor (Councillor F. R. Dixon Nuttall) took the chair. ELECTRIC LIGHTING. It was decided to apply to the Board of Trade for a provisional order to supply electricity for public and private purposes in the -borough. In reply to Mr. Kearns the Town-clerk said the limits of supply would be within the borough of St. Helens, and after powers were obtained the Gas Committee would consider what was necessary to be done. NEW BATHS FOR SUTTON. The Water Committee having been instructed to pre- pare plans for new baths at Sutton at a cost not exceed- ing £2,500. a tender was submitted for a building to cost £2,978, another with the omission of stone and other finishes brought it down to £2,783,and a third for a building reduced in Biz.> was £2,500. The Council be- ing asked to decide which plan they would adopt, went intoCommittee.—Mr. Greenough proposed that the highest sum for a good building be accepted. Colonel Gamble, C. 8., moved an amendment that the original sum of £2, 500 only should be spent, considering that ample accommodation would be provided for this sum. ' Plans on too elaborate a scale had been drawn. Instead of keeping within £2,500 the water engineer had drawn plans fora building to cost over £3,000. They had had this sort of thing over and over again, and he had al- ways protested against it, and had to protest against it still. The ratepayers had to pay, and he thought the Council should put their foot down and say they would , not exceed the sum sanctioned. Councillor Massey would discard these plans entirely and send the matter back. Colonel Gamble having agreed to withdraw his amendment, Mr. Massey's suggestion was put as an amendment, hut after a lively disussion was defeated , by 11 votes against 20 for the motion. 1 THE NEW LIBRARY FOR PARR, It was stated that on account of the illness of the Mayor of St. Helena, the opening of the new branch library at Parr had been postponed until next IVednes- day --Mr. Greenough complained that during the illness of tl e assistant at Sutton Library an application to the libra) ian at St. Helens for assistance had been disre- garded. The man had died that morning.—The Town Clerk said assistance had been sent.—The Deputy Chair- man of the Committee promised to inquire into Mr. Greenough's complaint. THE EXTENDED AREA OF THE noaouon. A proposition of the Paving Committee to have the new roads in the Denton's Green district (which leads trom the borough to the cemetery, and which form part of the area just included within the berough paved with stone setts, met with some opposition, but was finally adopted. DECREASE IN SCHOOL ATTENDANCE. Alderman J. C. Gamble called attention to the fact,' as shown in the statistics of the School Attendance Com- mittee, that the average attendance at th elementary , schools in the borough, had decireaged from 88 to 85 per ' cent., which, he said, wa s not a step in the night direc- tion.—Alderman T. THE FEATHERSTONE RIOTS.

-- REPORT OF THE COMMISSION. The report of the Committee appointed to inquire into the Featherstone riots was issued on Thurday night. The absence of the police at the Doncaster race meeting, it stated, left the district at the mercy of the mob. A large body of police might have proved sufficient to quell the disturbance at Acton Colliery ; but inasmuch as the military were ordered out it was a pity they were not met at Featherstone by a magistrate. The number of troops at the pit was so stria!l that it contributed to the danger, and perhaps rendered the taking of life neces- sary at last. The report recommended a more complete organisation of justice and civil power. It held that the conduct of the troops in firing on the crowd was justifi- able, for though an hour had not elapsed after the Riot Act was read, no officer could, in law, stand by and see outrage committed. The troops were in a position of great embarrassment. To withdraw from their position was to abandon the colliery to arson and violence. To hold the position was not possible except at the risk of the men being seriously hurt. In fact, at the supreme moment that the soldiers tired ,t heir action was necessary. The report suggested that some less dangerous weapons might be used by troops in quelling disturbance, and that it would be well to consolidate and codify the law with regard to the suppression of riot.

j NEWTON BOARD OF COMMISSIONERS.

The monthly meeting of this board was held at the Newton Town Hall an Monday. There were pre,sent, Messrs. J. W. Emmett (chairman), T. G. Ormard, P. McMahon, G. Ratcliffe, E. T. Evans, El. Cillin.(wood, P. Thompson, Worsley (law clerk), Brierley (clerk and surveyor). APOLOGY FOR NON-ATTENDANCE. The Chairman fa sd a letter of apology from Colonel McCorquodale arologising for non•attendance at the meeting of the Beard of Commissioners that day. THE WARDS QUESTION. A letter was read from Mr. R. Stone in which it was stated that the decision of the Commissioners with refer- ence to the proposed wards was not considered satisfac- tory, and informing the Commissioners that the commit- tee had resolved to petition the County Council to divide tile township into wards, with an equal number of representatives for each ward, and to increase the number of members from ten to twelve.—The Chairman said that he thought it was only fair to state why the Commissioners came to the conclusion they did with respect to the wards question. In the first place it must be remembered that the petitiostere scarcely represented one fourth of the ratepayers of the township. Therefore before they could give their deeision they would have to know the opinion of the other three fourths of the ratepayers. Another reason was that the Parish Coun- cils Bill was not yet through Parliament, and therefore the Commissioners were not in a position to jadge whether any alteration would be made. It would there- fore be premature to ask the Commissioners to say whether they were willing at once to have the township divided into wards. He had:read most of the Act, and it did appear to him that they had taken the right course. Whether the County Council had power to divide the township into wards he was not at present in a position to form a judgment upon. He could not see why the matter should be pressed just at present as there was nothing to be gained by it. Personally he had no objec- tion to dividing the township into wards, but they must first of all ascertain the opinion of the ratepayers upon the question. HIGHWAY AND SANITARY COMMITTEE. At a meeting of this Committee held on Nov. 20th, Mr. Brierley reported that on the 27th Oct., a person from Smithfield market, ha.d offered for sale in the lEarlestown market, decomposed rabbits, which on the following day were ordered by the magistrates to be destroyed. In consequence of some legal doubt in the proceedings, and on the instruction of the chairman of the Board, a compromise had been made in the accept- ance of fine and the purchaser of the rabbits refunded for his loss of time, whereupon Mr. Emmett gave notice that atthe Board meeting, he would move that the Pub- lic Health Act, 1890, be adonted for the district. A letter was read from the law clerk, on Mr. J. W. Thorpe's de- clining to pay the hospital charges of his manageress of Wellington Hotel. Resolved that the claim be main- tained, and that he be asked te pay one half of the costs, the offer to be made without prejudice to the claim. A letter dated 3rd Nov. was read from Mr. Thomas Smith on the condition of Tamworth-street. Resolved that he be informed that the commissioners in- tend sewering and pavir g the streets when they have funds available for the purpose, and that the street is used by other than commissioners' carts. A certificate from Mr. Beesley for £5OO due to Messrs. Neill and Sms on the Town Hall and offices at Earlestown was read. Resolved that a cheque be drawn for £3OO, the differ- ence being payments made by commissioners on speci- fied amounts in the contracts. MANCHESTER WESLEYAN GOSPEL MISSION.

MEETING AT EARLESTOWN. In aid of the rescue work which is being carried on in connection with the Central Hall Mission, a well attended meeting was held in the Wesleyan Chapel, Earlestown, on Thursday afternoon. Miss Watson presided over the tneeting and briefly in- troduced the various speakers, who were ladies en- gaged in the slums of Manchester, and who gave interesting details of their work in Connection with the mission. A colleetion was taken at the close, and the sum of 30$. was realised in aid of the funds. A number of those present, and others afterwards sat down to an excellent tea. , In the evening another meeting was held in the ' chapel, when there was again a fair attendance. Mr. Jno. Adams, Newton, presided, and after the meeting had been opened by singing and prayer, he referred to the object of the meeting, and said he had had a copy of the Rev. S. F. Collier's report for the past year, on the work which had been done, which was very interesting. From the report he learnt that right years ago a few God-fearing and philanthropic men started this Mission, at Manchester, arid it had gone on increasing year by year, and a large number of centres had been formed in the congested patia of the city, and it had proved a very great success indeed. On every Sunday they had as many as 10,100 people attending the'r services in differ- ent parts of the city, and Mr. Collier had siid that some- thing like 75 per cent. of these would not L ave attended any place of worship at all, had it not been for-tbe Mis- sion. The work they were carrying on was evangelistic, social, and mission work in the slums. They had a general opinion that in the slums ot Manchester, and other large cities, there was a great deal of poverty and crime, and he felt there was as much work to be done in the slums of the large cities, as there was, in connection with any of their foreign missions, and those who were doing the work, deserved all the sympathy, and practi- cal support they ccy•dcl give them. (Hear, hear.) They all knew that the population of this country was increas- ing at a very rapid rate, and from statistics it was proved that crime was decreasing, and it was owing to Mr. Collier's Mission, Dr. Barnardo's Home, Mr. St-venson's Home, and other institutions of a like nature. (Ap- plause.) Mr. Jones, a mission worker in Manchester, gave an interesting and humorous address in the course of which he interweaved details of the work which is being carried on in the slums. Sister Patty, one of the slum workers, also delivered an address, having par- ticular reference to her duties amongst fallen women. The meeting was very enthusiastic throughout, and at the close a vote of thanks to the chairman arid speakers was heartily accorded.

_ THE DEATH OF PROFESSOR TYNDALL.

ACCIDENTALLY POISONED BY HIS WIFE. The inquest concerning the death of Professor Tyndall was held on Thursday, and the evidence was pathetic. Mrs. Tyndall was examined, and said that in mistake she gave her husband a dose of chloral instead of sul- phate of magnesia. He noticed a curious sweet taste after he had taken the dose, and she then discovered, to her dismay, that she had given him chloral. When she told him, he said, " My poor darling, you have killed your John." Mrs. Tyndall in endeavouring to continue her statement with regard to the tragic incident, was ' overcome with emotion, and the coroner and jury WC P deeply affectel. Other witnesses spoke of the efforts to restore the Professor, and of his wile's devo- tion and gi ief, and a physician showed how prompt and skilful she had been in endeavouring to counteract the effects of the poison. A verdict of accidental death WAS returned, the jury expressing their siacere sympathy with Mrs. Tyndall.

THE SCOTCII STRIKE.

GOVERNMENT MEDIATION INVITED. Mr. Gladstome a,ked to sanction Government media- tion for the settlement of the miners' strike in Scotland, writes that the suggestion comes from one ride only. lie points out that in the English case both parties were be- fore the Government in the same attitude after repeat d efforts at conciliation. The moral influence of an Ad- ministration or a Minister, is not, he holds, an instru- ment to which it would be prudent to revert habitually in such a matter ; therefore it is necessary to pause befme assenting to the use of such influence un:ess a state of facts extstb analogous to that which made ti e action of Lord Rosebery practicable and expedient. Mr. Mundella, he adds, will send a competent officer into the district to it.quire into the eispute.
