A CHAT WITH MR. F. W. MONKS.

SOME INTERESTING STATEMENTS. The other day, writes a member of the " Examiner " staff, Mr. F. W. Monks kindly granted me an opportunity of interviewing him upon his voyage round the world, which he completed a fortnight to-day. Exposure to the extremes of many climes had had its effects upon Mr. Monks's fea- tures, and left them bronzed and weather stained. It was on the 18th of January when he left War- rington, to do what Mr. Cecil Rhodes calls" belting the globe,' and thus he was ten months away on the trip. Despite his hard travelling, however, Mr. Monks appeared to have satisfactorily fulfilled the objects in view. He combined business with plea- ' sure and succeeded in improving his health and ac- quiring much valuable information. "The first place," said Mr. Monks, 'which impressed me as being very striking, was Cairo. This was because of the pictur- esque appearance given to the plase by the mingling of almost every nationality. There were Egyptians, Europeans, Americans,Armenians,Turks, Soudanese and representatives of African tribes from as far West as Tangiers. All wear their national costumes, and, as you may imagine, create a most picturesque contrast. The climate is simply delighttul, and I look forward to paying another visit to Egypt, with pleasure. The Pyramids! oh, yes. I visited those grand monuments, and, like • others who have seen them, I was impressed by them and amazed that the huge blocks of stone of which they consist should have been placed into position by physical effort, when it would be a difficult task, to-day with our wonderful engineering appliances. There was one matter which surprised me, and that was the sharp dividing line which separates fertile from desert land. Wherever the Nile extends,there is luxuriant vegetation, and it is possible in many places to step off this fertile, grassy land on to the dry sandy desert. From Cairo I sailed via Aden, to Bombay." " You would observe something of the Anglo- Indian home life, in which so much interest is taken just nr,w, I suppose ?" "No ! Having no personal introductions and being so short a time in each place, I had no opportunity of gaining . access to the private houses. But there is no home life as there is here. The climatic condiaons are co different, you know. When the child- ren reach the age of six or seven, they have to be sent to Europe to be brought up. It wild never be possible to colonize there, as we do in more temperate regions. In my opinion the sooner we teach the natives to govern themselves, and leave them to manage their own affairs, the better it will be tor this country. Of course such a policy does not commend itself to Anglo-Indians for the very suffi-iient reason that they would be deprived of their offices." "The relations between the Anglo's and the natives—are they of a friendly character?" "Generally speaking I shou!d say so. In com- merce the natives are rather too much for our people, though. They are very cunning, and very fond of sharp practice.' "About your journey from Bombay to Calcutta, Mr. Monks." "I went to Calcutta via Delhi,Agra,and Luclinow, Sic. There was one point about the journey which struck me particularls and this was the congested condition of the passenger trains. Case after case I saw wfiere natives had to be left behind, because of the lack of accommodation. The engines and compartments are very similar to ours, except the first class compartments, invariably used by Euro- peans. In these the seats; are placed lengthwise, to permit of theie being turned into beds atnight. Only four persons as a rulenre placed in one compartment for this reason. Each passenger has t• supply his own bedding, but the inconvenience of this is not felt very much because one always, in India, travels with a man servant or "boy," who looks after these sort of matters. All through the North of India one sees an enormous number of Mohamedan mosques, the most interesting being at Benares. As we sailed down the Ganges there was a splendid view of these to be obtained. It was rather amusing, by the way, to see the number of natives who bathe in the river— there were hundreds of them to be seen. We also saw several native cremations along the banks. They are conducted quite publicly and in a very crude and simple fashion. The lyviy, quite uncased, is placed upon a heap of wood. The rich natives purchase scented wood for the purpose, but the poorer people use the wood they can procure cheapest." " Were you fortunate enough to see the carrying out of any native ceremony ?" "Nothing to speak particularly of beyond the Cremations." "What was your impression of Calcutta?" "Well the city is so large, and so beautiful that one could not attempt to ilescrihe it without care and trouble. It is certainly the city of palaces, al- though much of is present- beauty is the outcome of the European settlements. There is a matter which always impresses itself upon a luropean when visiting one of those large Indian cities. It is the nakedness of a great multitude of the natives. But one soon gets accustomed to it. It looses its strangeness because of the dark skins of the natives and the surroundings. After a short stay here I left for Rangoon." "Is Rangoon so unhealthy as it is generally des-

Oribed?" "No—at least the settlers led me to understand not. It may have merited thedeseriptionindaysgone by, but recently many sanitary improvements have been carried out in the town. The water supply is now obtained from splendid reservoirs, or tanks as they are called. Formerly the supply was obtained from the river, and from wells, and since this sys- tem has been abolished the town has been free of its old scourge—cholera. The drainage scheme has also been improved. I insp-cted it and found , Shone's ejectors in operation, and working very well. I left Rangoon for Colombo,in Ceylon. Here the vegetation grows in a most delightful and wonderful manner. Plants upon which we have to bestow the greatest care in our hot houses grow there to abnormal heights and form the hedge rows. They present sights so charming that to depict them by words would be an impossible task. Such plants, for instance, as the Sensitive plant, which we can hardly raise to nine inches, grow in the open to 15 feet and 20 feet. There are very fine botanical gardens here. "In what way are the ordinary in lustries carried on?" "Very much as they are in India. The nativea work in their own homes Some very fine silver work is done in Randy. And in a somewhat Peculiar fashion. If a man requires to have a silver article made, he first has to supply the 'raw' material in the shape of rupees which are smelted and then worked up. For this work the natives charge at the rate almost of half a rupee for every rupee they work up into a design." 'Upon leaving Ceylon where did you sail to." "I sailed next, to Australia. But I cannot say that I saw mnch scenery worthy of admiration. There an - peered to be nothing hut " scrub" and blue gum trees, and the landscape is somesch .t mono-011one, • " You were there at the time of the bank failures, I believe Mr. Monks ? " "Yes, I was in Adelaide and Melbourne at the time, and was surprised to see the calm philosophical manner in which the people took their misfortunes. One could almost have fancied they had been acquainted before- hand with what was going to happen and made every preparation." "What is your opinion of the financial crisis in that country " Well they have at present put the reconstruction I scheme into operation but I cannot help thinking that this is merely an expedient for giving a tem- porary relief. If there is a revival ;n trade, no doubt much of the danger impending upon other commercial houses will be retnoved The Anstritlians gamed to think they had passed the crisis, Nit I notice that, recently there have been a number of failures and I am afraid there ray he more." " How did you find tra.le in Melbourne, Mr. Monks ?" "'Twas very had indeed. There was practically no basiness being done. It was no doubt a sequel to the financial troubles. Many People in high stations in life had been sorely affected by losses. On every hand people were being, r,,rnmlied to retrench— Many of them compelled to sell their horses and carriages and dismiss their servantt, and live altogether in a smaller way." " Yes, and I suppose this sort of thing would extend to the artizan class ?" " Yes, decidedly. The question of what to do with the unemployed is as great a probkm as it is here. It is perhaps the zreatest question the kist ralians have to solve. At the present time the rate of toy to a labourer ' is Bs. a day for an eight boors workirg day. If the men would make COMP COrieeti,eoO it would no doubt enable the employers to pis ,vide more employ- But I am sorry to say, the men prefer

t ) live on their_ friends, _or upon what they can bee,

rather than modify their' demands. The sy,-t-em of pro- tection there is also, it appears to me, inflicting the greatest injury upon the country. They mac have been induced to go in for protection by the success which attended the system in the United States, quite overlooking the difference between the population, there being only 3,000,000 in Australiaagainst €4,000,000 in the States. You see the home cAssumption in America justifies their protective policy to same extent." "Did you observe much of the agricultural conditions

- of the country ?" " Not very much. What I did see • did not impress me very favourably. The land is very poor and certainly is in great want of water, From what I could gather it takes on the average two and half acres for the pasture of one sheep. Accordingly the exercise in covering the ground must be a little ag,ainss fattening. In England I believe the pasture required for two sheep is only one acre. The cities generally appeared to be in good sanitary order. At Melbourne, there is a depot rt t work very much like ours at Longford. I could not learn whether it paid or not because it had only been in operation a few

months." " In addition to Melbourne, Sydney,and Adelaide, you visited Brisbane I suppose, Mr. Monks." " Yes I did. The people there seemed to be still suffering from the effect of the floods which visited the city the previous March. And what a flood it must have been ! One could see some five feet up the lamp posts and walls, the mark of the water line. Even in shops and offices the water line was to be seen still. At the time of the flood a steamer which was moored in the river was carried and deposited by the inundation high anct dry upon the bank. Another flood came and lifted it back into the river. Two or three bridges were swept down by the combined forces of the rushing water and debris. Even when we sailed down the river there were to be seen along the banks clusters of ruined houses." "Did the chief cities impress you as possessing any particular characteristics ?" " Well, I was somewhat amused to see the fashions which held good in some of the leading cities. In Mel- bourne for instance, the people perspire under silk hats and frock coats, and follow up as nearly as possible the London fashions. In Sydney they are not so particular, and attire themselves in the more comfortable slouched hats and light cloths, whilst in Adelaidethe inhabitants look like the English country people." " What route did you take upon I, aving Brishaine 2" " Along the north coast to Java. This is the most pleasant voyage of all. It takes seveliteen days, and for onlytwo days were we outof sight of land,while crossing the Arafura Sea. Upon this voyage we saw the Pearl fisheries. The maritime hunters ot these shells are being

compelled to go more and more southward as the resources near the coast are almost expended. As they get into deeper water, and encounter greater dangers, the expenses increase the prices of the pearl shell." "Java! Yes, it is a beautiful place. Owing to the climatic conditions,the vegetation here is gloriously green all the year round. To visitors this is a charming fea- ture, but there is no doubt it becomes monotonous to residents. I could not help but think that were the Island held, by the English instead of the Dutch, there would be a greater yield from the remarkably rich soil. It is difficult for an Englishman to make himself under- stood here. At Buitecizorg there are magnificent botani- cal gardens. From Java, I went to Singapore. It is a very pretty place. The Chinese population is very large and it would appear that they are likely to push out the native Malay element. They are more industrious than the Malays, and save money from a wage which the natives find only sufficient to live upon. All the police here are drawn from India, chiefly Sikh. They manage the Chinese better than the Malays. From Singapore I sailed to Hong lE*ng, a beautiful place, rather small, well situated, and possessing a number of pretty gardens. Here in Hong Kong and other Chinese towns the natives are now obtaining their supplies from Japan, since tile exchange difficulty has set in and thus they avoid much trouble, as the currency of both coun- tries is the same. From Hong Kong we sailed up the river to Cantem, the dirtiest place I should imagine on earth. The streets are so narrow that one cannot travel in a carriage, and you have to be conveyed about in a sort of a sedan chair. As a matter of fact there is not a road in the whole of China wide enough to admit of a carriage being driven along it. As we proceeded in chairs through the town, the boys and girls took the opportunity to shout fankeei (foreign devil). On the other hand, the Coolies always seemed respectful, and took off their hats. I couldn't understand the reason for this marked respect, but..l found out the cause."

Mr. Monks smiled at the recollection, and be added" I noticed that the Coolies' hats were so broal that to savethem being knocked off by the sedan chair, they took them oil voluntarily. I was alluding to the Chinese as a commercial race. I was told by Europeans that they were the most upright people to deal with. Once they have contracted a bar- gain they will adhere to it even,at a serious loss to them- selves. They are very prompt at paying their bills. In- deed one of the customs of the country is that the natives consider it a religious obligation that th3yshould free themselves of debt by the c)mmencement of the new year. This they always strive to do. They are a dirty race of people though, and I can assure you the stenches with which the air in Caston was laden •eadered it necessary for us to clear out as soon as possible. Drains had been laid I think—some hunilr ed years ago—but apparently they had never beets touched since." " I rum Canton we sailed to Shanghai,where the streets are very wide and well kept, toe government of the town being in the hands of Europeans. English people go to London to see life ; Americans go to Paris. For the same reason the Chinese go to Shanghai. It is quite the thing for young Chinese noblemen to come from the in- terior and spend:a week or a fortnight in Shanghai. It serves them very much like London serves us, and in my mind there is no doubt that the natives are gradually having their eyes opened to the conveniences and advantages of Western civilization. There is a pretty public garden in the city, in which bands play five evenings dur- ing the week. Here the Europeans meet and as they are not compelled to wear wraps in the evenings, the scene is one of great pictur<squensss. There is no railway here. The Eurnpeans old endeavour to lay one to the mouth of the river, so as to have the port there, but the natives were offended and threw the rails into the river. We left Shanghai for Japan in one of the fine steamers of the Nippon Yusen Baisha. Arriving at Nagasoki, I was rather surprised to hear two Europeans say they had not seen a railway for 15 ant 18 years, while they had been in Chini." " There is a great deal of artistic work to be seen there of course ? "Yes, I think it is really the only country where a man is appreciated according to his ability in his trade. I was very much struck by the difference of the work for export and home purposes. The best artistic work is limited in,quantity and is bought by the

natives." " Is there any striking contrast between the Japanes3 and the Chinese." "Yes. The Japs if not cleanly are nothing, which cannot be said of the Chinese. The Japs take their 'tub ' twice a day. They are a very simple, childlike race, but are certainly clever at artistic work. The first impression we receive of them is that they are like grown up children. They are only small in statue—five feet I should bay is the average height. Much has been said about their adopting Euro- pean manners, but they are going back again to their original customs and dress, and very sensibly I think 1 they are acting in doing so. You can scarcely imagine the unbecoming and ridiculous sight they represent when dressed in European attire, especially when stand- ing by a European. They are Very economical and clean. 1 several times took off my shoes when entering their houses, rather than soil the floors. It is their custom to always take off their wooden shoes before entering a house." " From Japan I presume you would go to America 2" "Yes to Vancouver, and over the Rocky roe intains to Winnipeg and on to Chicago. Then into Canada, through ' Toronto, Quebec, and down the Hudson to New York. I was also at Boston and several other places. I think the country offers many better prospects for a young man who is able to go out with a small amount of capital. He has many better chances than in England. But at present trade is very bad there. When I visited Philadelphia the arCzan classes were in such straits that a relief fund had been started to sustain the people who were in many cases on the verge of starva- tion. Many large iron and steel works had not been running for two or three months and it seemed to me that the tariffs imposed by McKinley had had the effect of causing the production to go beyond the requir- ments of the country. The campetition was so keen that the ruling prices in iron and steel were below ours. Whenever the United States go in for free trade there is no doubt they will be able to compete with us in 9ther countries. I hope the day may be a long way ofrwhen this comes about. Wherever I have been I noticed how keenly the English trade was suffering from the Germans and Belgians. And in face of this, the policy lately adopted by the col- liers, it seems to me, is simply suicidal. If this were the only country from which coal, iron, and steel could be obtained, then I could understand the position taken up by the leaders of the strike. There is no doubt whatever that if we are to retain our trade in foreign markets our costs will have to be reduced." "How did'you find the feeling regarding Englisk sup- plies in most countries ?'' Australia and:othc r pl icea wherever the diffe- rence between our prices and continental prices was only 54., orders were always placed with English agents. When the difference was 7s. 6 I.per ton it became a mat- ter for consideration, but w here the difference is 10s.per ton, almost invariably the orders leave the English agent." "To revert to America again. Did you observe any- thing of their political life ?" " The chief thing that I noticed was that the Ameri- cans are becoming less the bigots of partizanship. They do not tie themselves t one party. If at one election the Republican policy seems favoarable, a voter will vote the Republican ticket, whilet at another election he may vote with the Democrats. I met quite a large number of persons who had done this at the last election. It seems to me to be a very geed sign."

"Did you call upon any Warzingtonians, Mr. Monks?" ." Yes I saw Mr. Whitham, at Worcester, Mass. He appeared to be in very good health. The only thing that was troubling him at the time was, as the staff at the works at which he was employed was being reduced, he expected to receive notice to leave. Before leaving the country huwever, I received word from him that he had not received notice." Mr. Monks afterwards made some allusion t the mannerism of the Americans. The voyage home in the Etruria was somewhat rough, owine, to the gale which had been blowing the previous week. The countries that appear to have impressed Mr. Monks most were Japan and China, plAces which he observed he whould like to study .more thoroughly.

THE EXAMINER, SATURD Y.. DECE

THE SHIP CANAL

A "GREAT FACT."

That the Ship Canal T;T:;-er-ea-t-f-a-ct " is now clearly recognised in Liverpool. At the meeting of the City Council on VVednesdarit was resolved to ask for a pro- visional order for the incorporation of several important Local Board districts which are now outside the city boundaries. If the scheme should be carried out the population of the city of Liverpool will be increased by more than 100,000 people. The scheme was advocated on the ground that the trade of the port was "threatened " by the Ship Canal, and it was necessary that the people of "Greater Liverpool " should unite " in protecting the trade on which they were all depen- dent."

THE CANAL AT RUNCORN. Dredging operations are being extensively carried on in the Ship Canal in the Runcorn district. Three vessels are at work, viz., the Mtnchester, the Barry, and the Walter Bibby, and a tremendous amount of material is being removed each day. The Walter Bibby is used in the construction of the Preston docks. Good progress is being made with the tidal opening at the Old Quay. The largest sailing ship which has come along the canal Irons Eastham arrived at Saltport on Friday last week. It is the Harvest Queen, 1943 tons burden, laden with timber from West Bay, America. The cargo consists of 2,643 loads of thnber, and is consigned to Messrs. Cross- field and Co., of Barrow. THE FIRST VESSEL ON THE SHIP CANAL. On Thursday, the first vessel sailed along the Ship Canal trim Eastham to Manchester. It had been arranged that the company's steamer Agnes seed Would .leave the Prince's Landing Stage Liverpool, with a party of directors on board, at an early hour, thence proceed- ing to Eastham, where the canal was entered. At East- ham the Snowdrop, one of the large Wallasey ferry boats was in readiness, and no time was lost in proceeding on the journey of inspection, the company on board includ- ing the principal directors and others, including Sir Jos. Lee, Sir John Harwood, and M:. Leader Williams, the engineer to the company, to whom the proceedings of the day, especially after the numerous difficulties which have been overcome, must have been very gratifying. The weather was bitterly cold, and a strong wind pre- veiled. Runcorn was reached about ten o'clock, and Acton Grange shortly before eleven o'clock. The turn- ing of the swing bridges in this district, notably at Stockton Heath and at Knutsford-road, was witnessed by a large number of people with considerable interest, but it was on arrival at the locks at Latchford that the greatest concourse of spectators was to be seen. The Snowdrop, which was followed by two steam tugs, reached the locks at a quarter-past eleven, and, amid a cheer, it left at twenty-five minute; to twelve on its way to Manchester. Amongst the spectators at Via locks, in addition to the officials of the compan7, may be men- tioned Mr. Alderman Wright, of Warrington, and Mr. T. Longdiu ',borough surveyor). The trio was in every respect a most successful one,and the directors expressed themselves much gratified with the successful comple- tion of the gigantic undertaking.

BANKRUPTCY OF A WARRINGTON STATIONER.—AIfred Downs, 1, church-street, stationer and news agent, appeared to undergo his public examination. Mr. P. The debtor had kept.no peeper books.and could not file a detailed deficiency account. Owing to the bad trade and ill-health, his household expenditure had exceeded his income. He was not aware of his insolvency wail last week, and aseerted that ha did not contract any liability with such knowledge.—ln answer to questions by the assistant official receiver (Mr. A. C. Potter) the debter said that he was first aware of his insolvency owing to a judgment which Mes-re. Rock Bros., Limited, gained against him. " What about the shop in Buttsrmarket-st., which he took five weeks ago ?" The debtor; " I took it to sell off some of my large Brock I had at the other shop. I thought I could sell off the stock. "AN ABOMINABLE CASE."—Joseph Robinson, Broom Edge, Lymm, baydealer, also appeared to go under his examination. His gross liabilitie3 amount to £l4l is. 11., with a deficiency of £135 133. Taz RECENT GALE.—The recent gale has caused a large amount of damage to the lists manned by the men from Penketh. One captain has lost his flat and two or three others have had very narrow escapes, one of them hav- ing been bound on the Welsh coast for nine days owing to the rough weather. TEMPERANCE LECTURE. —On Friday evening last week, a lecture in connection with the Wesleyan Band of Hope Society was delivered by Mr.J J. Jakemsn,of Liverpool, lecturer to the Temperance Socie•y. NOTES ON GAMES A.A)

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

" Splendicrall round exhibition" was how everyone described the League match, with Rochdale Hornets on Saturday. And in this case no scepticism of mine led me to disbelieve this general opinion. There was tine trifling disadvantage in connecticn with the match, however. This was owing to the presence of a person whose manners cast a chilling influence over both spectators and players. I allude to John Frost, Esq. This person had also got the ground in such a firm grip the" bone" was only knocked out of it after some 300 of those quiet Warrington youths, who frequent the ground, had been practising upon the arena for half an hour. For some time, however, there was some doubt as to whether the fixture should be brought off, owing

to this unpleasant condition of the field. However, this obstacle was overcome and all danger of accidents occurring was removed by the prompt manner in which the referee followed up the tackling.

One noticeable feature about the match was the bril- liant play of the visiting forwards ; it was play which was admired alike by their oppments and by the spectators. Despite this advantage, however, the home backs were simply impregnable; those brilliant forward dashes of the Insects produced nothing more than a buzz of applause—no scores. The try scored by Taylor during the first half was, indeed, a brilliant effort, and was another instance of the capital abilities possessed by this young, though popular member of the club. The second half of the game enabled Warrington to c )ntrast their staying powers with the somewhat limited capaci- ties of endurance possessed by their opponents. The aggressive play during this period was entirely on the Warrington side. This would have meant a consider- able augmentation of the score had the energy been followed up by the necessary amount of finish.

The home forward play wag chiefly represented by Jolley, Taylor, and Bailey. The Bates were both in good form again, and at times their play simply stag- gered their opponents. This excellent form was greatly facilitated by thvir unselfish passing. Fleesom, who filled Boscow's post again was in good form and his de- fensive play was a great factor in enabling Warriitgton to make a win of the game.

ANTECEDENTS.

The subject of our antecedents" this week is the sterling forward of the Warrington Club, William Ran- dies. This player first saw the light in Orford-lane, in the year 1862 so that he is a Warringtonian bred and born, and is 31 years of age. Although born in the Orford end of the town, he has lived the greater part of his life in the salubrious (?) district of Bank Quay, and is engaged at the Soap Works of Messrs. Crosfield.

It is as a footballer chiefly, that Handles is known. His first experience at " leather hunting " was with the Warrington True Blues, who played in a field off Liver- pool-rd., which club he joined in the year 1879, after- wards transferring his services to the Warrington Rovers who played off Lovely-lane. In these two junior clubs, which were prominent clubs in their time, he was a three-quarter back. His abilities were recognised by the football authorities of the Warrington club, and in jubilee year, 1887, he was chosen to play as threequarter back for the A team.

After playing at tbreequarter back, he was drafted into the" shoving "department of the A team, and so well did he play among the forwards that in the next season, he was promoted to the premier team with which he has played with very few exceptions since. In fact during the seven years he has been playing with the club he has scarcely missed a match. Amongst the playing members of the club, he has been playing the longest period, with the exception of Turner and Jolley. The forward division is not a place in which individual play is usually very prominent, and he must be a most excellent player who shines in an extraordinary decrree during a match. Randles, however, has done a good deal of hard work, and does not shirk his work in the scrimmages. He has done a considerable amount of scoring during his time, which shows that he is pos- se>sed with good judgment in utilising his chances.

In other branches of athletics, he is not so prominent as in football, hut he is a fair cricketer, although he has not played for the Warrington team. His ambition has not riFen higher than playing in the junior clubs, amtangst which has been Bank Quay Missien,and Trinity clubs. He is not a mean antagonist at bowls, and has played in several matches prcmoteci amongst the Soap works employes by Mr. A. H. Crosfield.

NEW BOOKS, &c.

Oae of the Cranford series of books recently issued by Macmillan and Co. is "Hood's Humourous Poems," a reprint of all that is best of Hood's lighter verse, the quality of which is so well known and appreciated. It is capitally illustrated by Charles E. Brock, the preface being written by Alfred Ainger. A nursery story for very little children called " Mary," by Mrs. Molesworth,(Macmillan) is one of the books sure to be in demand for Christmas presents. With a num- ber of pictures to stimulate the interest which is always felt in Mrs. Moleeworth's books, "Mary ".cannot fail to please. Two complete stories fill "Christmas Arrows," the Quiver annual. Evelyn Everett-Green contributes the fir t(" Hilda York,") a very interesting and well written story, and the Rev. P. B. Power, MA., supplies the second. The first part of a new volume begins with " Cassell's Family Magazine" for December, a magazine as popu- lar as any and as deservedly so. The "Cabinet Portrait Gallery" for the same month gives three beautiful cabinet photographs ; one, a group artistically represent- ting the King and Queen of Dmmark, the Princess of Wales, the Duchess of Fife, and the Lady Alexandra Duff, the other two being splendid portraits of Sir Henry Norman and Mr. Santley. The principal literary feature of the Christmas number of "The Century Maeazine," is the beginning of Mark Twain's serial novel (" Pudd'nhead Wilson,") with por- trait of the author. The opening chapters promise well, I and the remaining stories, illustrated articles, Sze., are quite up to their usual high standard of excellence. We have received from the publisher, (John Heywood) a seasonable book with a seasonable title, "A Basin o' Broth." The broth is composed mainly of Lancashire herbs, and the herbs are tales, rhymes, and sketches made by Teddy Ashton and Co., including. Capanbells, Ben Adhem, and the Gravedigger. As the name suggests the sketches, &c., are mostly in the Lancashire dialect, and are cleverly written, in addition to being humour- ous and entertaining. They are very suitable for public reading and reciting. "Teddy Ashton's 'Xmas Annual" is a similar hook en a smaller scale' with the almanac and weather forecasts for 1894 included. Another of Messrs. Saxon and Co.'s indispensable six- penny books is " Everybody's Letter Writer," being a complete guide to letter writing, and containing the correct method of addressing letters in all possible eases, I adapted to private, social, commercial or political uses, with illustrations. For excellence of reading mattcr, variety of contents, I and the high tone of its selections, the Christmas num- ber of "Great Thoughts" deserves a hearty word of commendation. Two richly coloured presentation plates " The Good Shepherd," and " Mary's Offering are included, with a number of complete stories and short extracts in prose and verse. One of the best, if cot the best sixpenny journals for ladies is "The Gentlewoman." The issue for Dec. 2nd contains a portrait of the Mayoress of Warrington (Mrs. Greenall), alottg with a number of the new Mayoresses of the British Isles. On the opposite page is a portrait of a wide-awake, charming baby—The Lady Alexandra Duff ;—the entire number is refined and varied; alto- gether an excellent one. " Sylvia's Home Journal" is another well-conducted, comprehensive journal which caters exclusively far the fair sex. The Christmas number presents its readers with an artistic design of portion of a band for portiere or piano back, and book cover in ribbon embroidery.

WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY AND THE

GYMNASIUM. Sir,-1 shall be glad if you will afford me an oppor- tunity of correcting a wrong Impression which I an afraid has been created by the statement made by Mr. Aitken in your issue of Saturday last. His letter implied that the Gymnasium Committee are wanting in sym- pathy with the Society he represents, and had peremp- torily refused the use of the building for the concert to be held on Tuesday evening next. This is not so, and Mr. Aitken's application was carefully considered, with a desire to assist him if possible, but as the members had already been put to inconvenience through the erection of the necessary stage for purpose of rehearsal, and further, by closiug of the Gymnasium for a week during the late performance by the Amateur Dramatic Society, the Committee did not consider it would be wise fa the interests of the institution to further encroach on the goodwill of its members, seeing that their fees were payable in advance This was tully explained to Mr. Aitken, and also, that as the building was not yet provided with necessary seating, great difficulty would arise in the arrangement for so important a concert. I trust under the circumstances the public will give the Gymnasium Committee some little credit for having en- deavoured to meet Mr. Aitkan's wishes, and so help him out of the difficulty to which. be referred.—l am, yours faithfully, ..... _

THE COAL WAR.

Sir,—lt is wonderful what an army of friends the poor collier is blessed with,and it is surprising how they differ in their mode of friendship. In the first place, there is the great army of workers who have been suffering with their wives and little ones, and yet murmured not one word of censure, because they knew that the collier was fighting in the first rank for the cause of labour, so that the workers might have a living share of the profit of his labour, and, thank God, they have won a noble victory. Then, again, there is another very numerous class of friends, especially when the collier is in full work and a bit free with his money, and they are those little petty shop-keepers, and a host of others of the same kind,who are never satisfied unless they are making cent. percent. on what they sell to the collier and other workers who trade with them. But, if the collier, or any other worker, should rebel against their masters' tyranny, they are terrible in their denunciations, not because they have any personal dislike to the collier, but because their tak- ings are not as large as they usr d to be. Then, again, there is another numerous class of friends, and they are those renegades whom trades unionists call blacklegs. Now, if " Cymro "had been working among colliers all his life time, and, as he says that his wife is a collier's daughter, he must know that the collier deserves all he can get, if he would only consider the risk he has to undergo. But it is a question of £s. d. with Cymro." And, I am sorry to say, that there is a itTultitude of the same type who are for ever lecturing, the colliers and other workers who have the courage to stand up for a wage sufficient to feed and clothe their wives and children. Then " Cymro " goes on to say that it is no secret that they are selling steam coal at Oldham for lls, per ton, brought from Swansea in South Wales, which is bought at Swansea for ss. . Now I don't like:to say that " Cymru "is uttering an untruth, but I am afraid • that he is stretching the facts, for the following reasons :—(1) That the coal in the neighbour- hood of Swansea is all house coal. (2) All the steam coal that is shipped at Swansea has to be brought from Aberdare and the Rhondda Valley, and I am certain that he cannot buy it at the pit mouth for ss. per ton, without speaking of the carriage to Swan- sea. Now, it happens that I belong to that town and know what I ant talking about. Then, Cymro "says that he has yet to learn that the Govern- ment has a legal right to the minerals of the country. He says they might have a moral right. But Igo fur- ther than him and say that we have a right to all the natural mineral resources of our country. And then, again, I think that it is a monstrous shame that some fifty persons should have it in their power to paralise the whole of the industries of our country because up- wards of 300,000 working men would not submit to work in a coal pit for a starvation wage so that the fifty might have the more to squander at the gambling table or the race course. Now, Sir, if the workers cf this country were wise, and would act wisely, they could very soon alter tke little game of trying to starve the colliers and others, who are willing to work, hut want to by sending a fair representation of labour into the House of Commons to uphold the rights and dignity of the workers in general. Remember, fellow-workers, there are none who know your wants like men of your own class. I hope you will for the future take no heed of the plaintive cries of " Cymro." and his kith, as they are very much like so many dogs in the manger. " Cymro " should understand that man requires something more than work in this old world ; he requires time for self- culture, both mental, and physical, also for the society of his wife and children. But he cannot do this if he de- pends on " Cs-mro's " reasoning, and is to be ruled by the arbitration of the capitalist. In conclusion, I woul.l say that he who would be free himself must strike the blow.—Yours &c., 33, Wiowick-street. Warrington.

J. PRICE.

THE ETHICS OF INDUSTRIALISM.

Sir,—Perhaps you will allow me a few more inches of space to reply to Mr. John Peers on the labour question. In my last letter I exposed the hollowness of Mr. Peers' argument with regard to intimidation by the Miners' Federation. To argue that, because a few colliers used violence to and intimidated others, the whole of the men constituting the Miners Federation must be described as " tyrants," is about as magnificent an example of men- tal confusion as I have ever come across. But John really improves in his mixing of things as he goes along. There is a devil-ma'-care recklessness about his building of an argument that cannot fail to amuse even if it does not convince. For instance he says that every man has a right to sell his labour in the best market at the best price possible. Of course "every man "means a unionist as well as a non-unionist. Therefore the members of the Miners' Federation had the right to get the best possible price for their labour. But not only have they a right to do this, but this right " must be rigor- ously enforced and upheld." These are the words of Mr. Peers, but no sooner has he laid down this proposition than he proceeds at once to demolish it. And this is how he does it. He objects to see the chemical workers of Widnes marching ta Preseot Workhouse and back for 81. a day to enable the colliers to fight out their dispute with their masters, a dispute, mind you, which was not begun by the miners, but by the coalowners, of whom it is absurd to say as Mr. Peers himself declares, that they have a right to minerals which they have never seen. Very well, my opponent's argument is that the men have a right to sell their labour at the hest possible price; and that they have not the right to sell it except on the coalowners' terms, because other men would be thrown out of work Will John kindly oblige in your next issue by reconciling these two obvious contradictions. And will he also be good enough at the same time to show how the right of the miners to the best price can be' rigorously enforced and upheld," except by ceasing', to work, and sending the chemical workers t'o Winston to work for eight-pence a day. I wonder if it ever occurred to you Mr. Peers why a body of chemical workers who create vast wealth, should he compelled to avail them- selves of vvhistonian advantages, when miners or some other workers are " rigorously enforcing " their rights. " It will he a bad day for England when she accepts the precept that you may do evil in order that good may come to yourself." So says Mr. Peers, but I am sorry to say he is too late. Industrial England has not only accepted the precept, but practises it every day. It's a sad thing to say, but there is no use beating about the bush ; we may as well admit it at once. But what I object to is, and what really prompted me to enter into this discussion was, that Mr. Peers and " Cymro " should wink at the evil done by the coalowners and presume to lecture the minera for exercising what under our present competitive system is their undoubted right. Just listen to this statement made so reeently as last Saturday—" Turn where one may one finds the modern habit of thought to be that capital is first and labour nowhere. The shareholders of a company which has been capitalised by promoters at twice the just value of the business look upon their' rights' to dividends as a sacred pos- session, and they are generally willing that the labourer should starve rather than that their demand should be foregone. The demands af the capitalist have been sus- tained by preacher and economist alike to the utter oblivion of those of the capital-maker. So a demand for a living wage is a crude expression of revolt against this neglect. The worker is coming forward to say, We must have our share in the benefits which are accruing to Inankiud from the increase in labour-saving inven- tions, and In the power of men to control the forces of nature.' Hitherto the capitalist—be he an honest pro- vider of money or a fraudulent ' promoter ' or large handed waster of millions [This is where Mr. Peers' talisman " thrift " would come in, I presume] raised for purposes in themselves, and to a moderate extent legi- timate—has intercf pted nearly all the profit which the progress of invention has brought to mankind." And further : "Once and attain I have gazed on the squalor and degradation in which tens of thousands of the labouring classes of this country live, and have won- dered how these people did not at once lie down and

die." These are not the utterances of a fanatic like my- self John, and whethpr they will lead to anarchy and bomb-throwing I cannot say. They are the deliberately written words of the city editor—the money and com- mercial editor—of that eminent!y respectable Tory organ, the London "Standard." I shall look neat week for my opponent's further attempt to bolster up the moral position of his friends, the capitalists. It needs it.— Yours, kc.,

"COMMON JUSTICE."

DRINK AND LABOUR.

Sir,—The events of the past few weeks touching on the drink question seems to call for a few words from me. do not wish to set myself up as an authority,but I have an earnest desire to do something towards liber- ating my fellow-men from a thraldom that carries along with it, a thousand and one plausible excuses. Having had some experisnce of the labour question, and having been much in the company of my fellow labourer, both in his serious, and in his jovial humours, I have had to come to the conclusion that to drink, directly, and in- directly has he to look for the cause of all his woes. In the recent elections, Warrington has acted no worse than scores of other towns throughout England, and it is of no use turning round and rating the workingman for his stupidity. All the little petty-fogging " causes " which are floated,ancl to which he is invited,are so much waste of time, and of money. It is well-known that the man who spends a larger portion of his earnings in drink, does not attend these meetings. Another thing is well-known, especially to those who, like the writer, have been a pretty good at- tender of the pub ! A pretty large percentage of the topers are good scholars, and constant readers of the " Daily." When sober, these men will average a higher general intelligence than those who are dealing out the goody-goody stuff at social unions and such like. They will, many of them, confess to you that they know as well as you do that such an one as keens a Black Horse, or a Bay Horse pub is not to be compared with the other man who is a manufacturer. He knows that the one who gives employment to a heap of people in turn- ing out a fine piece of leather, is a better member of society than the man who turns hops and other miscel- laneous articles into beer. What then ? How is it, you will say, if he knows al this, why does lie act so stupidly ? Well, I will give my opinion about it. He is a good deal of a pessimist, and he don't know it. He has come to the conclusion that the strongest portion of the social fabric is against him, and it is no use struggling. He knows that the Bible, tells him strong drink is not for princes, but that it is for" him that is ready to perish," and for those who are of" heavy hearts." Your columns have recently borne terrible evidences as to the number of heavy hearts in Warrington. I suppose if the heavy hearts of the coal centres,and of the mill, and factory centres,could during the past few months have been a public view, what a chamber of horrors tire' would have made. Tom Mann has recently said something about the churches. What he said about hypocrisy we believe to be true. If we are wrong, so be it. Churches and employers do not come within the workman's list of friends. The former is a grand, and stately fabric, with a stand-of-ish air about it. The latter lie knows nothing of. It has lost its personality, making an appearance now and again behind two stately horses. The workman will never forget the taking of a penny in the shilling to pay the extra coal bill. He is now silently hoping it may be as Dead Sea fruit in the mouths of those who take it. A few more failings have employers, and church-goers,and I leave them to be dealt with by John Peers who will, no doubt, avail himself of the opportunity when he has done patting the" scab " on the back. The drink question will never be a buried question so long as its ending, or mending, is left in the hands of the churches. It will never be an unknown quantity so long as it remains in politic. The public-house exists because the public demands it, unfortunately. Public sentiment and law on the one side, and the selfishness of capital on the other, make it what it is. 'The more you oppress " the trade" the more it retaliates and de- generates. Until the public is convinced that it is an evil, all attempts to repress it must fail. Remove it from politics by placing it on the same level with other commercial enterprises. Remove it from all taxation, by offering licenses to all who conduct law-abiding places. Encourage a better class of men as keepers of public-houses. See that all drinks are pure and with- out chemics. What I have said is the result of much study of the drink problem. It is worthy consideration. Not one of the fads already on trial, nor yet one of those aimed at by the temperance party, has the ghost of a chance of bringing satisfaction to anyone so long as beer, bible, and politics, put money in the pockets of the few. PAUL BERT.

POLEMICAL AMENITIES.

" But were the Middle Ages less cruel than Pawnees, Hebrews, ROMP ns,or Assyrians ? Obviously the inedirs- val tortures inflicted on political prisoners, witches. and heretics were as nefarious as any known to ancient or savage conquerors. Nothing is changed but the vic- tims. Then you find Covenanters torturing witches, Episcopalians in England or Scotland torturing Jesuits, wizards, or Catholics at large. You find Covenant-ra burning naughty little boys alive. Yeu find William and Mary just as ready with the " boot " as James II was. Then the burning of witches slowly dies out as the edu- cated Joss becomes sCept:caI."—ANDREW LANG. Sir,—l am indeed sorry that Mr.J C.Dalton should have taken my attempt at mediation so much amiss. I was quite sincere in wishing to moderate the furore of wordy schismatic war. To this end I suggested to these jealous champions a common platform from which, instead of mauling each other in •a cause alike in badness, they might turn their weapons to use against a common enemy from this common platform. But my idea did not seem to "take on." Each combatant finds it easier and more congenial, to fling mud at the other dug from the ruts in which they prefer to stick. I am sorry for this. It is painful to see two estimable gentlemen thus illustra- ting the fable of the black and white sided shield. Both sides are right, and both wrong—inasmuch as both their churches have been sinners in intolerance and persecu- tion. Mr. Dalton'a side has been the greatest sinner in this respect, because it has had the greater opportunity. Human nature has a deal to do with these things. I presume both these gentlemen were born in the faith they so zealously defend. Like cabbages, where they are planted there they stay. Had the fates so ordained it they would have been equally zealous Mussulmen or Brahmins. This being so, I think it a pity to see them thus wasting their energies while a common enemy is attacking the very citadel of their faith. So much on the general aspect of the question. Now I beg to offer a few remarks for the further consideration of Mr. Dalton. As this gentleman has entered upon this discussion I presume he is not averse to hearing a plain statement on the other side? In his nostrils the word rationalist stinks. At this I have no cause to wonder. His creed has ever had the strongest aversion to reasoned personal judgment, and has, wherever possible, persecuted it to the death. The slight acquaintance of our critic, with this " reasoned judgment " or rationalism is fully I evinced by his having to go back a whole century to rake up an adverse example of the intolerance of ration- 1 alism. And to misname a frenzied mob—wild with the excitement of having so recently broken loose from the galling bondage of a grinding tyranny—to call the unreasoning madness of the French Revolution—ratiow alism is surely a misuse of terms. This is not the rationalism of Hume, of Leckv, Huxley or Spenser.. It was a quarrel among the Catholics, in which the mob sidoi arrainst the clerics, as having aided and abetted their tyrants in their oppressions. It speaks volumes in tavour of rationalism that Mr. Dalton would get no nearer for a home thrust. Why are not the sects flying at each others throats—rending each other to pieces— burning and torturing. as was their wont a little time ago P Why are we even now, not obeying the injunc- tion so strictly enjoined upon us in certain documents of " not suffering a witch to live " ? Because we are get- ting to be more reasonable—more reasoning— more rational more Rationalistic ! Will Mr. Dalton kindly tell us what other caur,e can have produced an beneficial a change ; while we have the Scriptural injunction still staring us in the face. Is not obedience to this injunction as imperative as ev it was ?—Yours, &c.,

JOHN PEERS.

Sir,—Under the above heading in; yours of Saturday last, Mr. J. C. Dalton advances the oft-repeated and fallacious assertion. "In every case where sanguinary punishments have been inflicted for heresy (!) it was the civil authority which passed sentence and inflicted punishment, and the Catholic (Papal) Church obliged the ecclesiastical judge to plead to the civil power for pardon for the culprit." Mr. Dalton must think your readers very ignorant if they cannot see through such an absurd argument, as though the judge who passed sentence on the innocent Protestant, must afterwards Plead for his aequittal. Why' then did the Council of Constance in 1415 order John Huss and Jerome of Prague to be burnt to death ? Why, sir, it was because they taught "The sufficiency of the Scriptures as being the only safe rule of faith, the bread and wine to be taken by clergy and laity alike, at the Lord's Supper, etc," whereas this very Council had decreed that the wine must he withheld from the laity, ordaining," that every heretic who should dare to advance any contrary sentiment, should he banished and tortured by the In- quisition." Verily the tender mercies of the Papacy are cruel ! I wish also to point out an error in one word of my last letter. The word " defence " in the quotation from Dean McNeile should be " defiance." I advise Mr. Peers to study the system cf Popery before he again rushes into print," Cramp's Text Book" or' Rainsford's or Hyslop's Two Babylons " are standard books. Super- stition and ignorance join hands in the Papacy. ROBERT FOULKES. Frodsham, Dec. 5, 1893.

Rigby told him on ti* date named to " kneck two or three of Walker's wag' gone in the iron si,ling." tie asked him whether the were to go first,ancl tie understood Rigby to answer yes' He saw Rigby give the signal to the drives, and he turned the points for the iron siding. Witnesi said he made an inspection of the waggons standing iU the iron siding before he turned the points, as was ill! duty, but in cross examination by Mr. Forshaw mitted that he did not dose, and didn't notice the cart, being laden at the siding.—Thomas Morton, engine,, driver, and Albert Leigh, fireman, gave evidence as" the speed the engine shunted.—Richard Jenkins, Or. ter for Messrs. Care and Ridgvrav said he went with tbe deceased will his cart to the Dallain-lane siding. Theft had almost finished loading their carts, when, withouf any warning being being given to them, a numbene coal waggons WPI e shunted with considerable force. (witness) did nit receive much injury, but he folio' Hanson right between two waggons, the buffers of t," waggon being smashed off. It was with great ditn' culty, the deceased was got from between the tvre waggons. He was afterwards taken to the Infirmarrs Aftera short consultation, the jury-returned a verdie° of" Accidental death " caused wholly through the 10 competency of the servants employed by the Londog and North-Western Railway Company.

THE MINERS' FEDERATION. • MINERS AND "CON CRACTING OUT." The Conference of the Miners' Feleration of Great Britain was opened on Thursday, in Birmingham. Mr. I E. Cowey presided, and there was a large attendance of I I delegates. The busimss in relation to the Gonciliatirm Board,which includes the appointment of representatives and the discussion of rules, was deferred. It was reported that there were nine thousand Federation miners still out of work, and it was decided to continue the levies. A very confidsnt hope was expressed that the strike at the Scotch pits would terminate early next week. A long discussion took place on the Employers' Liability Bill. Reference wa.ff made to the deputation which waited on Lord Salisbury, ostensibly representing the Miners' Permanent Belief Society, to urge the l'ory leaderto see that the power of the contracting out clause was introduce& Ths Lancashire dt legates emphatically denied that this deputation._ represented the miners. It : was asserted that the bulk of the pitmen of this county were distinctly opposed to any such arrangement. Mr. T. Aspinwall moved a resolution to the effect that tee deputation was not authorised to speak on tx,half of the miners, and that the Conference protested against the principle of contracting out of the provisions of the bill. The resolution was adopted, as was also another declar- ing that no bill would be satisfactory which permitted masters or men to contract out in any circumstances or for any consideration..
